, and state it slightly more generally to allow us to apply this theorem with matrices
S
which are not sign matrices. We give a proof for completeness.

Let
\widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
such that
\|SA\widehat{X}-SB\|_{F}\leq(1+\tau)\min_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
. In addition, define
\widetilde{X}:=\argmin_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
. Finally, let
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be an orthonormal basis for the column span of
A
. As in
[
CW09
]
, first let us show that
\|A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\leq O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}
. We can upper bound
\|A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}
as follows:

\begin{split}\|A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}&=\|UU^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\|U^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\|U^{T}S^{T}SU\cdot U^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})+(I-U^{T}S^{T}SU)\cdot U^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\|U^{T}S^{T}SU\cdot U^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\|(I-U^{T}S^{T}SU)\cdot U^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\|U^{T}S^{T}SU\cdot U^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\|I-U^{T}S^{T}SU\|_{2}\|U^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\|U^{T}S^{T}SA(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\frac{1}{3}\|A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\end{split}

(7)

Here, the first inequality is by the triangle inequality, and the second inequality is by
\|CD\|_{F}\leq\|C\|_{2}\|D\|_{F}
with
C=I-U^{T}S^{T}SU
and
D=U^{T}A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})
. The last inequalitiy holds with probability at least
1-O(\delta)
by our first assumption on
S
. Rearranging gives

\begin{split}\|A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\leq\frac{3}{2}\|U^{T}S^{T}SA(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\end{split}

(8)

Thus, it suffices to obtain a bound on
\|U^{T}S^{T}SA(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}
. First, observe that by the normal equations for the problem
\min_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
,

U^{T}S^{T}S(A\widetilde{X}-B)=A^{T}S^{T}S(A\widetilde{X}-B)=0

and therefore,

U^{T}S^{T}SA(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})=U^{T}S^{T}S(A\widetilde{X}-B+B-AX^{*})=U^{T}S^{T}S(B-AX^{*})

Combining this with Equation
8
and using the fact that
S
has the
(\sqrt{\varepsilon/k},\delta)
approximate matrix product property, we find that

\begin{split}\|A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}&\leq\frac{3}{2}\|U^{T}S^{T}S(B-AX^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{k}}\|U\|_{F}\|B-AX^{*}\|_{F}\\
&=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\|B-AX^{*}\|_{F}\end{split}

(9)

Here the second inequality holds with probability at least
1-\delta
since
S
has the
(\sqrt{\varepsilon/k},\delta)
approximate matrix product property, and the first equality is because
\|U\|_{F}=\sqrt{k}
.

Next, let us bound
\|A(\widehat{X}-\widetilde{X})\|_{F}
. By the Pythagorean theorem and the normal equations for the problem
\min_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
,

\|SA\widehat{X}-SB\|_{F}^{2}=\|SA\widetilde{X}-SB\|_{F}^{2}+\|SA(\widetilde{X}-\widehat{X})\|_{F}^{2}

Therefore,

\begin{split}\|SA(\widehat{X}-\widetilde{X})\|_{F}^{2}&=\|SA\widehat{X}-SB\|_{F}^{2}-\|SA\widetilde{X}-SB\|_{F}^{2}\\
&\leq(1+\tau)\|SA\widetilde{X}-SB\|_{F}^{2}-\|SA\widetilde{X}-SB\|_{F}^{2}\\
&=\tau\|SA\widetilde{X}-SB\|_{F}^{2}\\
&\leq\tau\|SAX^{*}-SB\|_{F}^{2}\\
&\leq\frac{\tau}{\delta}\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}\end{split}

(10)

Here the first inequality is because
\widehat{X}
is a
(1+\tau)
-approximate solution to the problem
\min_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
. The second inequality is because
\widetilde{X}=\argmin_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
. Finally, the third inequality holds with probability
1-\delta
by Markov’s inequality and the fact that for any matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times c}
,
E[\|SM\|_{F}^{2}]=E[\Tr(M^{T}S^{T}SM)]=\Tr(M^{T}M)=\|M\|_{F}^{2}
, where we used the fact that
E[S_{i}^{T}S_{j}]
is
1
if
i=j
and
0
otherwise.

In summary,

\|SA(\widehat{X}-\widetilde{X})\|_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{\tau}{\delta}\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}

with probability
1-\delta
, and since
S
is a subspace embedding for
A
with probability
1-\delta
, this implies that

\|A(\widehat{X}-\widetilde{X})\|_{F}\leq O\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\tau}{\delta}}\Big)\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}

Thus, by the triangle inequality,

\begin{split}\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}&\leq\|A(\widehat{X}-\widetilde{X})\|_{F}+\|A(\widetilde{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq O\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\tau}{\delta}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\Big)\|AX^{*}-B\|\end{split}

(11)

Finally, by the normal equations for the original problem
\min_{X}\|AX-B\|_{F}
, the columns of
A
are orthogonal to those of
AX^{*}-B
, and by the Pythagorean theorem,

\begin{split}\|A\widehat{X}-B\|_{F}^{2}&\leq\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}\\
&\leq O\Big(\sqrt{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\frac{\tau}{\delta}}\Big)^{2}\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}+\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}\\
&\leq(1+O(\varepsilon)+O(\tau/\delta))\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}\end{split}

(12)

with probability
1-O(\delta)
. Taking square roots gives

\|A\widehat{X}-B\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{1+O(\varepsilon)+O(\tau/\delta)}\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}\leq(1+O(\varepsilon)+O(\tau/\delta))\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}

as desired.

We additionally show that this holds if
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a random matrix whose entries are each chosen from
\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\}
uniformly at random, with
m=O(k\log(1/\delta)/\varepsilon)
. The subspace embedding property holds due to the following lemma (which can be applied with
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{3}
):

Lemma 8

(Lemma 3.4 of
[
CW09
]
)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon,\delta>0
, and
m=O(k\log(1/\delta)/\varepsilon^{2})
. There is an absolute constant
\eta>0
such that if
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has entries from
\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\}
(which are at least
\eta(k+\log(1/\delta))
-wise independent), then for any
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
with orthonormal columns, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
\|U^{T}S^{T}SU-I\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon
.

Remark 6
.

Note that there is a typo in Lemma 3.4 of
[
CW09
]
, which should say
m=O(k\log(1/\delta)/\varepsilon^{2})
. This is because it applies Theorem 2.2 with
\|A\|_{F}=\|B\|_{F}=1
, and
\delta
replaced by
\frac{\delta}{\eta^{k}}
for some constant
\eta>1
. This does not affect the overall proof, since when applying Lemma 3.4, it is enough to take
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{3}
.

In addition, the approximate matrix product property is due to the following theorem from
[
CW09
]
:

Theorem 13

(Theorem 2.2 of
[
CW09
]
)
.

Let
\delta,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times a}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times b}
. Then, for
m=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)/\varepsilon^{2})
and
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
having entries drawn uniformly at random from
\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\}
(which are at least
C\log(1/\delta)
-wise independent for some constant
C>0
),
\mathbf{Pr}\Big[\|A^{T}S^{T}SB-A^{T}B\|_{F}\leq\varepsilon\|A\|_{F}\|B\|_{F}\Big]\geq 1-\delta
.

∎

We will also use the following version of the above result to perform multiple-response
\ell_{2}
regression, with sketching matrices other than dense sign matrices. This version will be used when we want very low failure probability (and thus cannot afford the
O(\tau/\delta)
term in the approximation error) but do not need
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate solutions of the sketched problem to remain good solutions.

Theorem 14

(Essentially Theorem 3.1 of
[
CW09
]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1/2)
. Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d_{1}}
,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d_{2}}
, with
A
of rank at most
k
. Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a random matrix which has the
(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{k}},\frac{\delta}{3})
approximate matrix product property, and is a
(1\pm\frac{1}{3})

\ell_{2}
subspace embedding for
A
with probability at least
1-\delta
. If
X^{*}=\argmin_{X}\|AX-B\|_{F}
, and
\widehat{X}=\argmin_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
, then with probability
1-O(\delta)
,
\|A\widehat{X}-B\|_{F}\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Theorem 3.1 of
[
CW09
]
, with slight modifications — we give the proof for completeness. First, if
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
is an orthonormal basis for the column span of
A
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,
\|U^{T}S^{T}SU-I\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{3}
(by the definition of subspace embedding). Now, let
\widehat{X}=\argmin_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
. As before,

\begin{split}\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}&=\|UU^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\|U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\|U^{T}S^{T}SU\cdot U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})+(I-U^{T}S^{T}SU)\cdot U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\|U^{T}S^{T}SU\cdot U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\|(I-U^{T}S^{T}SU)\cdot U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\|U^{T}S^{T}SUU^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\frac{1}{3}\|U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\|U^{T}S^{T}SUU^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\frac{1}{3}\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\end{split}

(13)

Here the second inequality is because
S
is a subspace embedding for
U
. Thus,

\begin{split}\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\leq\frac{3}{2}\|U^{T}S^{T}SUU^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}=\frac{3}{2}\|U^{T}S^{T}SA(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\end{split}

(14)

where the equality is because
UU^{T}A=A
. Now, by the normal equations for the sketched problem
\min_{X}\|SAX-SB\|_{F}
,

U^{T}S^{T}S(A\widehat{X}-B)=A^{T}S^{T}SA(\widehat{X}-B)=0

meaning that

U^{T}S^{T}SA(\widehat{X}-X^{*})=U^{T}S^{T}S(B-A\widehat{X})+U^{T}S^{T}SA(\widehat{X}-X^{*})=U^{T}S^{T}S(B-AX^{*})

Therefore,

\begin{split}\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}&\leq\frac{3}{2}\|U^{T}S^{T}SA(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\frac{3}{2}\|U^{T}S^{T}S(B-AX^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{k}}\|U\|_{F}\|B-AX^{*}\|_{F}\\
&\leq\frac{3\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2}\|B-AX^{*}\|_{F}\end{split}

(15)

Here the second inequality holds with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{3}
since
S
has the
(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{k}},\frac{\delta}{3})
approximate matrix product property. Thus,

\begin{split}\|A\widehat{X}-B\|_{F}^{2}&=\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}\\
&\leq O(\varepsilon)\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}+\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}\\
&=(1+O(\varepsilon))\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}\end{split}

(16)

Here the first equality is by the Pythagorean theorem together with the normal equations for the problem
\min_{X}\|AX-B\|_{F}
. This completes the proof.
∎

We will apply the above lemma with sketching matrices of the form
S\times I
where
S
is a dense sign matrix. To show that
S\times I
is a subspace embedding we will use the following lemma:

Lemma 9
.

Let
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
, and let
n_{1},r_{1},s_{1},n_{2},r_{2},s_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose
S\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{1}\times n_{1}}
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon_{1})

\ell_{2}
subspace embedding for
U
. In addition, let
V\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}\times r_{2}}
and suppose
T\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{2}\times n_{2}}
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon_{2})

\ell_{2}
subspace embedding for
V
. Then,
S\times T
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon_{1})(1\pm\varepsilon_{2}

\ell_{2}
subspace embedding for
U\times V
, i.e.

\|(S\times T)(U\times V)x\|_{2}=(1\pm\varepsilon_{1})(1\pm\varepsilon_{2})\|(U\times V)x\|_{2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}r_{2}}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}r_{2}}
, and let
X\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}}
so that
X_{ij}
is equal to the entry of
x
in the coordinate
(i,j)
. Then,

\begin{split}\|(S\times T)(U\times V)x\|_{2}=\|(SU\times TV)x\|_{2}=\|SUXV^{T}T^{T}\|_{2}\\
=(1\pm\varepsilon_{1})(1\pm\varepsilon_{2})\|UXV^{T}\|_{2}=(1\pm\varepsilon_{1})(1\pm\varepsilon_{2})\|(U\times V)x\|_{2}\end{split}

(17)

where the third equality is because
S
is a subspace embedding for
U
, and the fourth equality is because
T
is a subspace embedding for
V
. This proves the lemma.
∎

In addition, we will need the fact that dense sign matrices have the JL moment property.

Lemma 10

(Sign Matrices Have JL Moment Property — Lemma 2.3 of
[
CW09
]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
, and let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have entries which are i.i.d. and uniformly random in
\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\}
. If
m\in\Omega(\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}})
, then
S
has the
(\varepsilon,\delta,\log(1/\delta))
-JL moment property.

Proof.

By Lemma 2.3 of
[
CW09
]
, if
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a rescaled dense sign matrix, then for any unit vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
p\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left(\mathbb{E}[|\|Sx\|_{2}^{2}-1|^{2p}]\right)^{1/p}\leq O\Big(\frac{p}{m}\Big)

Thus, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left(\mathbb{E}[|\|Sx\|_{2}^{2}-1|^{p}]\right)^{1/p}\leq(\mathbb{E}[|\|Sx\|_{2}^{2}-1|^{2p}])^{1/(2p)}\leq O\Big(\sqrt{\frac{p}{m}}\Big)

Thus, if
m\geq\Theta(\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}})
, then for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left(\mathbb{E}[|\|Sx\|_{2}^{2}-1|^{p}]\right)^{1/p}\leq O\Big(\varepsilon\sqrt{\frac{p}{\log(1/\delta)}}\Big)

Thus,
S
in fact has the Strong
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-JL Moment Property (see Definition 19 in
[
AKK+20
]
). By Remark 1 of
[
AKK+20
]
,
S
therefore has the
(\varepsilon,\delta,\log(1/\delta))
-JL moment property as long as it has at least
\Theta(\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}})
rows.
∎

A.2.5
Kronecker Product Regression in
\ell_{2}
Norm

Recall that Tucker-
q
decomposition is equivalent to finding
q
factors
U^{1},\ldots,U^{q}
and a core tensor
G
such that
\|(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{q})\text{vec}(G)-A\|_{2}^{2}
is minimized. Note that if the factors
U^{1},\ldots,U^{q}
are known, then this is an
\ell_{2}
regression problem, where the design matrix is a Kronecker product of
q

n\times k
matrices. This
\ell_{2}
regression problem can be solved very efficiently:

Theorem 15

(
\ell_{2}
Kronecker Product Regression — Theorem 3.1 and Algorithm 1 of
[
DJS+19
]
)
.

Let
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{q}
, where
A_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d_{i}}
. Let
n=\prod_{i}n_{i}
and
d=\prod_{i}d_{i}
. Let
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, there is an algorithm which, in running time
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\text{nnz}(A_{i})+\text{poly}(d/(\varepsilon\delta))
and with success probability
1-\delta
, returns
\widehat{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q})\widehat{x}-b\|_{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\min_{x}\|(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q})x-b\|_{2}
, and also returns
\widehat{e}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\|(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q})\widehat{x}-b\|_{2}-\|b\|_{2}
(where we use the notation
a=(1\pm\varepsilon)b
to indicate that
a\in[(1-\varepsilon)b,(1+\varepsilon)b]
).

Proof.

This follows from Theorem 3.1 in
[
DJS+19
]
with minor modifications. The main difference is that we desire an algorithm which also returns an estimate of the error
\|(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q})\widehat{x}-b\|_{2}
, in addition to returning the solution
\widehat{x}
itself. To achieve the desired result, it suffices to show that if
D
is a leverage score sampling matrix as generated by
[
DJS+19
]
, then it gives a weak
\varepsilon
-affine embedding, as defined in Theorem 7.10 of
[
CW17
]
. Observe that if
D
has
\text{poly}(d/(\varepsilon\delta))
nonzero entries, then it is a subspace embedding (as shown in Theorem 2.11 of
[
Woo14
]
). In addition, by Proposition 3.6 of
[
DJS+19
]
,
D
satisfies the approximate matrix product property with error parameter
\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}}
with probability
1-\delta
, as long as
D
has
\text{poly}(d/(\varepsilon\delta))
nonzero entries. Thus, both of the hypotheses in Theorem 7.10 of
[
CW17
]
are satisfied by
D
with probability
1-\delta
, meaning that with probability
1-\delta
,
S
is a weak
\varepsilon
-affine embedding for
(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q},b)
, i.e., for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\|D(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q})x-Db\|_{2}-\|Db\|_{2}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\|(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q})x-b\|_{2}-\|b\|_{2}
. Thus,
\widehat{x}
can be found using the pseudo-inverse of
D(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q})
as in
[
DJS+19
]
, and
\widehat{e}
can be computed as
\|D(A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q})x-Db\|_{2}-\|Db\|_{2}
. Note that the running time needed to compute
\widehat{e}
is
\text{poly}(d/(\varepsilon\delta))
.
∎

A.2.6
\ell_{1,2}
-norm Low Rank Approximation

Several algorithms for
\ell_{1,2}
-norm low rank approximation are given in
[
CW15
]
. Based on the techniques of
[
CW15
]
,
[
JLL+21
]
also gives an
O(1)
-approximation algorithm for
\ell_{1,2}
low rank approximation with bi-criteria rank
k\cdot\text{poly}(\log k)
. The following is a modified version of the result presented in
[
JLL+21
]
, with the OSNAP matrices given in Theorem 9 of
[
NN13
]
replaced by the sparse embeddings with
O(\frac{d\log d}{\varepsilon^{2}})
rows given in Theorem 4.2 of
[
Coh
]
.

Theorem 16

(Based on Theorem 1 of
[
JLL+21
]
, Theorem 4.2 of
[
Coh
]
)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, there is an algorithm which outputs
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times O(k\log^{2}k)}
and
V\in\mathbb{R}^{O(k\log^{2}k)\times d}
, such that
\|UV-A\|_{1,2}\leq O(1)\min_{B\text{ rank }k}\|B-A\|_{1,2}
. The running time of this algorithm is
(\text{nnz}(A)+d^{2})\cdot k\cdot\text{poly}(\log k)
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of Theorem 1 in
[
JLL+21
]
, with the sparse embedding matrix
S
in the proof of that theorem now being a sparse embedding matrix with
O(k\log k)
rows, rather than the large
k\cdot\text{poly}(\log k)
as in Theorem 9 of
[
NN13
]
. Note that the bicriteria rank is
O(k\log^{2}k)
since the number of rows in
S
is
O(k\log k)
, and the number of columns in the Lewis weight sampling matrix
S^{\prime}
(in the proof of Theorem 5.8 of
[
JLL+21
]
) thus has to be
O(k\log^{2}k)
, in order for
S^{\prime}
to give an
\ell_{1}
subspace embedding for the row span of
SA
.
∎

Appendix B
Approximation Algorithm for Tree Networks

In this section, we consider the following problem:

Problem 3
.

Let
k,n,q\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{T}=(V,E)
be a tree, with vertices
V
and edges
E
, with
|V|=q
. In addition, suppose that for each
v\in V
,
U_{v}
is a tensor with
\deg(v)+1
modes. Here, for each edge
e
incident to
v
,
U_{v}
has a mode of dimension
k
, and
U_{v}
has one additional mode of dimension
n
that is not connected to any other tensor. Finally, define
\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
to be the
q
-mode tensor obtained by contracting the tensors in
\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\}
in the manner specified by the tree
\mathcal{T}
. Then, given a tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
and a tree
\mathcal{T}=(V,E)
, we wish to find
U_{v}
for
v\in V
minimizing
\|\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}
.

Since the input of the algorithm includes
\mathcal{T}
, a question that one may ask is
how to instantiate our algorithm for tree network decomposition
— i.e., how to choose the tree
\mathcal{T}
and the corresponding rank
k
given a tensor
A
. We note that one potential way to do this is by considering whether
A
has low rank according to a more general tensor network (i.e., a graph which may have cycles) — we define contraction of general tensor networks in Definition
11
. This also answers the question of
why tensor contraction networks may be of interest
in addition to other notions of decomposition such as CP, Tucker, and Tensor Train decomposition. For instance, the Tensor Ring decomposition on
q
modes
[
ZZX+16
]
corresponds to a cycle with
q
vertices and aims to overcome various limitations of Tensor Train decompositions, such as the asymmetry of the dimensions of the first and last modes versus the internal ones. Also, the Projected Entangled Pairs States (PEPS) network is used as an ansatz for quantum wavefunctions, i.e., the ground state of two-dimensional Hamiltonians, see, e.g.,
[
Orú14
]
. The work of
[
YL18
]
discusses general tensor networks further, and gives examples of tensors
A
which have low Tensor Ring rank but much higher Tensor Train rank. In terms of the space complexity used to represent a network, a sparse network might only need
O(q\cdot n\cdot\text{poly}(k))
parameters, while other decompositions could use space which is exponential in
q
. In Section
C
, we give an algorithm which, given a tensor
A
, a graph
\mathcal{G}
and a target rank
k
, outputs a tree
\mathcal{T}
and a tensor
M
which has rank at most
\widetilde{O}(\frac{qk^{\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G})}}{\epsilon})
according to
\mathcal{T}
, such that
\|M-A\|_{F}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{T}\|T-A\|_{F}
, where
T
ranges over all tensors which have rank
k
with respect to the graph
\mathcal{G}
.

B.1
Warm-up: Improving Previous Results of Bicriteria Algorithm for Tensor Train Decomposition

First, we describe the Tensor Train decomposition algorithm of
[
HSW17
]
. Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
is the input tensor. Then, in
[
HSW17
]
,
U^{q}
is found as follows. First,
[
HSW17
]
defines a sketching matrix
G\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{q-1}\times s_{q}}
, which is instantiated as a Gaussian matrix (but as we discuss below, could also belong to any other family of oblivious sketching matrices which has the affine embedding property). Then, the rows of
U^{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{q}\times n}
form an orthonormal basis for the column span of
M_{q}(A)G
. Finally,
A
is replaced by
A\circ U_{q}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n\times s_{q}}
(i.e., the first
q-1
modes are
n
-dimensional).

In general, to find
U^{i}
for
i\geq 2
,
[
HSW17
]
proceed as follows. Suppose
U^{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{q}\times n}
and
U^{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{j}\times n\times s_{j+1}}
(for
j
between
i+1
and
q-1
) have already been found, the rows of
U^{q}
form an orthonormal basis, and the rows of
M_{1}(U^{j})
form an orthonormal basis for
j
between
i+1
and
q-1
. Furthermore, suppose that at this point
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n\times s_{i+1}}
(and
A
now has
i+1
modes). Then
U^{i}
is computed as follows — first,
G\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{i-1}\times s_{i}}
is again an oblivious sketching matrix (which
[
HSW17
]
instantiate as a Gaussian matrix). Then, the rows of
M_{1}(U^{i})
form an orthonormal basis for the column span of
M_{\{i,i+1\}}(A)G\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{i+1}n\times s_{i}}
. In addition, once
U^{i}
is found,
A
is replaced by
A\circ(U^{i})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n\times s_{i}}
(i.e.,
A
has
i
modes, and in the operation
A\circ(U^{i})^{T}
, the last two modes of
A
are contracted with
(U^{i})^{T}
). Finally, after
U^{2},\ldots,U^{q}
have been found,
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s_{2}}
, and
[
HSW17
]
defines
U^{1}:=A
. This completes our description of their algorithm.

Turning the Algorithm of
[
HSW17
]
into a
(1+\varepsilon)
-Approximation Algorithm.

In
[
HSW17
]
, it is shown that this is a
\sqrt{q-1}
-approximation algorithm, with a running time of
O(qs^{2}\cdot\text{nnz}(A)+qs^{3}n)
, where
s=\max_{i\in[q]}s_{i}
— note that the bicriteria rank is also
s
. However, with a different analysis, one can in fact show that the algorithm of
[
HSW17
]
is a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm, with bicriteria rank
r=\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon)
and
O(qs^{2}\cdot\text{nnz}(A)+n\cdot\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon))
running time — we discuss one such analysis now.

First, suppose
U^{1}_{*},\ldots,U^{q}_{*}
form the optimal Tensor Train decomposition of rank
k
for
A
. Then,
(U^{q}_{*})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
is the solution to the multiple-response
\ell_{2}
regression problem

\min_{U}\|UM_{q}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q-1}_{*})-M_{q}(A)\|_{F}

Then, one can multiply on the right by a sketching matrix
G_{q}
which is an
\frac{\varepsilon}{q}
affine embedding for
M_{q}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q-1}_{*})
and
M_{q}(A)
with failure probability
O(\delta/q)
, meaning that for all
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,

\|UM_{q}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q-1}_{*})-M_{q}(A)\|_{F}=(1\pm\varepsilon/q)\|UM_{q}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q-1}_{*})G_{q}-M_{q}(A)G_{q}\|_{F}

Then, if
(U^{q})^{T}
is defined to be
M_{q}(A)G_{q}(M_{q}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q-1}_{*})G_{q})^{\dagger}
(or equivalently, if
(U^{q})^{T}
is defined to be an orthonormal basis for the column span of
M_{q}(A)G_{q}
, and the change of basis matrix
(M_{q}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q-1}_{*})G_{q})^{\dagger}
is contracted with
U^{q-1}_{*}
) then
U^{q}
increases the error achieved by
U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q}_{*}
by a factor of at most
(1+O(\varepsilon/q))
. Note that this step is exactly as in the algorithm of
[
HSW17
]
, which lets
U^{q}
be an orthonormal basis for the row span of
G_{q}^{T}M_{q}(A)^{T}
.

Now, suppose
U^{q},\ldots,U^{i+1}
have been found. Then, fixing the factors
U^{q},\ldots,U^{i+1}
and
U^{1}_{*},\ldots,U^{i-1}_{*}
, the optimal solution
U^{i}
with the other factors fixed is the solution to the regression problem

\min_{U}\|U(M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})\times M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q}))-M_{i}(A)\|_{F}

Now, multiplying
A
by the sketching matrix
G
, as done by
[
HSW17
]
, corresponds to multiplying the matrices in the above objective on the right by
(G\times I)
, and the new objective is

\min_{U}\|U(M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})G\times M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q}))-M_{i}(A)(G\times I)\|_{F}

The sketching matrix
G
can be selected so that this does not change the objective by more than
(1\pm\varepsilon/q)
— this can be achieved by selecting
G
to be an affine embedding for
M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})
and
M_{\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(A)^{T}
, since for all
U\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n\times k}
,

\begin{split}\|U(M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})&\times M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q}))-M_{i}(A)\|_{F}\\
&=\|M_{1}(U\circ U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})^{T}M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})-M_{\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(A)^{T}\|_{F}\end{split}

(18)

and moreover,

\begin{split}\|U(M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})G&\times M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q}))-M_{i}(A)(G\times I)\|_{F}\\
&=\|M_{1}(U\circ U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})^{T}M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})G-M_{\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(A)^{T}G\|_{F}\end{split}

(19)

Thus, the approximation factor is only increased by a factor of
(1+O(\varepsilon/q))
if
U^{i}
is chosen to be

U^{i}:=M_{i}(A)(G(M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})G)^{\dagger}\times M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})^{\dagger})

We can see that this definition of
U^{i}
is equivalent to the definition of
U^{i}
in
[
HSW17
]
, due to the following:

•

Note that multiplying
M_{i}(A)
by
G\times I
is equivalent to the usage of
G
in
[
HSW17
]
.

•

In addition, the key point is that the rows of
M_{i}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})
are orthonormal, due to
U^{i},\ldots,U^{q}
being orthonormal bases. Thus, the pseudoinverse of
M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})
is
M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})^{T}
, and therefore, multiplying
M_{i}(A)(G\times I)
by
(I\times M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})^{T})
is equivalent to contracting
A
with
(U^{q})^{T}
, then with
(U^{q-1})^{T}
, up to
(U^{i})^{T}
. This is exactly as is done in
[
HSW17
]
.

•

Finally, note that the change of basis matrix
(M_{i}(U^{1}_{*}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1}_{*})G)^{\dagger}
can simply be folded into
U^{i-1}
, and this is equivalent to letting
U^{i}
be an orthonormal basis as is done in
[
HSW17
]
.

Thus, at each iteration of
[
HSW17
]
, if the sketching matrix
G
is chosen properly, then
U^{i}
as defined in
[
HSW17
]
is in fact a
(1+\varepsilon/q)
-approximate solution to the
\ell_{2}
regression problem defined by
U^{q},\ldots,U^{i+1}
and
U^{1}_{*},\ldots,U^{i-1}_{*}
.

Our
(1+\varepsilon)
-Approximation Algorithm for Tensor Train Decomposition with Improved Running Time

The running time of the algorithm of
[
HSW17
]
(with the sketching matrix
G
modified appropriately to obtain
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation) is
O(qs^{2}\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon)
, where
s=\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon)
is the number of rows in
G
, and is also the bicriteria rank of the algorithm. We now show that this running time can be further reduced to
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon)
using our subspace embedding for matrices of the form
M_{\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(U^{1}\circ\cdots\circ U^{i-1})
described above.

In the algorithm of
[
HSW17
]
, the main reason for the
O(qs^{2}\cdot\text{nnz}(A))
running time is that in each iteration,
A
is contracted with
(U^{i})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{ns_{i+1}\times s_{i}}
— thus, as stated in
[
HSW17
]
, before this
\circ
j is performed,
A
has at most
s_{i+1}\cdot\text{nnz}(A)
nonzero entries, meaning that the time needed to perform this
\circ
is at most
O(s_{i}s_{i+1}\cdot\text{nnz}(A))=O(s^{2}\cdot\text{nnz}(A))
. Furthermore, after this
\circ
is performed,
A
has at most
s_{i}\cdot\text{nnz}(A)
nonzero entries, as mentioned in
[
HSW17
]
. As we mentioned above, the contraction of
A
with
(U^{i})^{T}
is needed as a step in computing the pseudo-inverse of
M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})
and contracting it with
A
.

In order to improve the running time, we note that computing the pseudo-inverse of
M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})
is in fact not required, and a weaker guarantee suffices — that is, it suffices to obtain a subspace embedding for the row span of
M_{1}(U^{i}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})
. Note that we in fact need our sketch to be an affine embedding for
M_{1}(U^{i+1}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})
and
(G\times I)M_{i}(A)
, and we can inductively prove that this holds for our map
\mathcal{L}
that we defined earlier in this subsection. We want to show that for all
X
of the appropriate dimensions

\|XM_{1}(U^{i+1}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})-(G\times I)M_{i}(A)\|_{F}=\|XM_{1}(U^{i+1}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})\mathcal{L}-(G\times I)M_{i}(A)\mathcal{L}\|_{F}

Suppose
S_{q},\ldots,S_{i+1}
are the sketching matrices used when applying
\mathcal{L}
to
M_{1}(U^{i+1}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})
— then,
\mathcal{L}
can be written as
(S_{q}\times I)(S_{q-1}\times I)\ldots S_{i+1}
(where the identity matrices
I
are of the appropriate dimensions). It suffices to show that the
(S_{j}\times I)
are each affine embeddings with error
O(\varepsilon/q^{2})
and failure probability
O(\delta/q^{2})
— however, by reshaping appropriately, this just holds as long as
S_{j}
itself is an affine embedding with error
O(\varepsilon/q^{2})
and failure probability
O(\delta/q^{2})
.

The key point is that this removes the
s^{2}
factors in front of the running time. Note that we can reuse the
S_{j}
, i.e., when trying to find
U^{i}
, we can reuse the
S_{q},\ldots,S_{i+2}
which were used when trying to find
U^{i+2}
. Thus, in the iteration where
U^{i}
is computed, it suffices to simply contract
A
with
S_{i+1}
, which takes
\text{nnz}(A)
time if
S_{i+1}
is a Countsketch matrix (see
[
Woo14
]
for more information on Countsketch matrices). Over the course of
q
iterations, this amounts to a running time of
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))
instead of
O(qs^{2}\cdot\text{nnz}(A))
. Once we have applied
\mathcal{L}
, we will compute the pseudo-inverse of
M_{1}(U^{i+1}\circ\cdots\circ U^{q})\mathcal{L}
— however, this is simply a
k\times\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon)
matrix, meaning that we only incur an
n\cdot\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon)
term in the overall running time as a result of this computation. Thus, the overall running time for Tensor Train decomposition when using our new subspace embedding is
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon)
.

B.2
Bicriteria
(1+\varepsilon)
Approximation Algorithm for Tree Networks

We obtain a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm with bicriteria rank
\text{poly}(qk/\varepsilon)
. The key idea of our algorithm is as follows. We root the tree arbitrarily. Then, we move from the leaves to the root of the tree, computing factors
U_{v}
for nodes
v
in that order. We compute the factor
U_{v}
for a node
v
once all of the factors for its subtree, excluding
v
itself, have been computed — aside from this, the order in which we process vertices can be arbitrarily chosen. To allow for efficiently computing
U_{v}
given the factors in its subtree, we carefully apply Countsketch matrices in succession. We analyze Algorithm
3
below.

Algorithm 3

(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for tree network decomposition with output rank
O(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon}\log(\frac{q}{\delta}))
. Note that we can assume each of the leaves in
\mathcal{T}
has one mode of dimension
n
— if a leaf
v
has no such mode, then
U_{v}
is simply a
k
-dimensional vector, and can be contracted with its parent and ignored for the purposes of approximating
A
.

A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
with
q
modes, and a tree
\mathcal{T}=(V,E)
with
O(q)
vertices, such that for any
u,v\in V
with
(u,v)\in E
, the edge between
u
and
v
has rank
k
, and each vertex in
V
has at most one mode of dimension
n
. Without loss of generality, each leaf in
V
has one mode of dimension
n
. All vertices of
\mathcal{T}
have degree at most
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\}
such that for each
v\in V
, the dimension of
U_{v}
corresponding to each of its outgoing edges in
\mathcal{T}
is at most
t=O(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon}\log(q/\delta))

// Process the leaves first

L\leftarrow
A list of all the leaves of
\mathcal{T}

for

v\in L

do

T_{v}\leftarrow
An
O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})\times n^{q-1}
Countsketch matrix

R_{v}\leftarrow
A
t\times O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})
matrix whose entries are drawn i.i.d. from
\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\}

i\leftarrow
the mode of
U_{v}
of dimension
n

\widetilde{U_{v}}\leftarrow M_{i}(A)T_{v}^{T}R_{v}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times t}

// Store a sketch of
\widetilde{U_{v}}
, which we denote
M_{v}
, for future use.

S_{v}\leftarrow
An
s\times n
Countsketch matrix, where
s=O(\frac{q^{4}t^{2}d^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})=O(\frac{q^{6}k^{2}d^{3}}{\varepsilon^{4}\delta}\log^{2}(\frac{q}{\delta}))
.

M_{v}\leftarrow S_{v}\widetilde{U_{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times t}

A\leftarrow S_{v}\circ_{i}A

end

for

// Now process each vertex
v\in V
after processing its entire subtree.

\mathcal{I}\leftarrow
A list of the vertices in
V\setminus L
where each vertex appears after the rest of the vertices in its subtree.

for

v\in\mathcal{I}

do

// First compute
\widetilde{U_{v}}
using the sketches of the subtrees of the children of
v
.

M_{v-subtree}\leftarrow\bigotimes_{u}M_{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{\deg(u)-1}\times t^{\deg(u)-1}}
, where
u
ranges over the children of
v

\mathcal{G}_{1}\leftarrow
The modes of
A
corresponding to row dimensions of
M_{u}
for the children
u
of
v

\mathcal{G}_{2}\leftarrow
The remaining modes of
A

\widetilde{U_{v}}\leftarrow M_{v-subtree}^{\dagger}\circ_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}A

if

v
is not the root of
\mathcal{T}

then

T_{v}\leftarrow
An
O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})\times n^{|\mathcal{G}_{2}|}
Countsketch matrix

R_{v}\leftarrow
A
t\times O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})
matrix whose entries are drawn i.i.d. from
\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\}

\widetilde{U_{v}}\leftarrow R_{v}T_{v}\circ_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\widetilde{U_{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{t\times\ldots\times t\times t}

end

if

// Next, compute
M_{v}
.

M_{v}\leftarrow M_{v-subtree}\circ\widetilde{U_{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{s^{\deg(u)-1}\times t}
where the contraction is along the modes of
\widetilde{U_{v}}

corresponding to the edges between
u
and its children

S_{v}\leftarrow
An
s\times s^{\deg(u)-1}
Countsketch matrix.

M_{v}\leftarrow S_{v}M_{v}

A\leftarrow S_{v}\circ_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}A

end

for

return

\{\widetilde{U_{v}}\mid v\in V\}

Theorem 17
.

Let
k,q,n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
A
and
\mathcal{T}
be as specified in Algorithm
3
. Then, Algorithm
3
finds
\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\}
with bicriteria rank
t=O(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon}\log(\frac{q}{\delta}))
such that

\|\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\min_{U_{v}}\|\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}

with probability at least
1-\delta
. The running time of Algorithm
3
is
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(d)}
.

Proof.

For convenience, let
U_{v}^{*}
for
v\in V
be such that

\|\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}^{*}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}=\min_{\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\}}\|\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}

and the dimension of
U_{v}^{*}
is
k
along the modes corresponding to the edges incident to
v
(along with potentially one mode of dimension
n
). Throughout the proof, we will let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of vertices
v\in V
for which
\widetilde{U_{v}}
has already been computed so far — in our proof, we show by induction on
|\mathcal{S}|
that with probability
1-O(\frac{|\mathcal{S}|\delta}{|V|})
,

\|\mathcal{T}(\{\widetilde{U_{v}}\mid v\in S\}\cup\{U_{v}^{*}\mid v\in V\setminus\mathcal{S}\})-A\|_{F}\leq\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}\Big)\Big)^{|\mathcal{S}|}\|\mathcal{T}(\{U^{*}_{v}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}

Note that
A
changes throughout the course of Algorithm
3
since we contract it repeatedly with Countsketch matrices — for clarity, in this proof, we let
A
denote the original value of
A
provided at the time of input, and we let
A_{v}
denote the value of
A
when we are attempting to compute
\widetilde{U_{v}}
.

Let
w\in V
be the vertex that is currently being processed (i.e. if we are in the step where we process the leaves in
L
, then all the leaves prior to
w
in
L
have been processed, and otherwise if
w\in\mathcal{I}
, then all the vertices in
\mathcal{I}
before
w
have been processed). Assume that

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{T}(\{\widetilde{U_{v}}\mid v\in S\}\cup\{U_{v}^{*}\mid v\in V\setminus\mathcal{S}\})-A\|_{F}&\leq\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}\Big)\Big)^{|\mathcal{S}|}\|\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}^{*}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}\end{split}

(20)

Without loss of generality, assume that
w
is not a leaf of
\mathcal{T}
— the case where
w
is a leaf is similar but simpler. Then, the optimal
U_{w}
(with
\widetilde{U_{v}}
for
v\in S
and
U_{v}^{*}
for
v\in V\setminus(S\cup\{w\})
held fixed) is the minimizer for the multiple-response
\ell_{2}
regression problem, defined as follows.

First, let
\mathcal{T}_{above}
denote the tree which is obtained by removing the subtree rooted at
w
from
\mathcal{T}
, and let
V_{above}
denote the vertices of
\mathcal{T}_{above}
. In addition, let
\mathcal{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{T}_{\deg(w)-1}
denote the subtrees rooted at each of the children of
w
, and let
V_{i}
denote the vertices of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
. Then,

\begin{split}\mathcal{T}(\{\widetilde{U_{v}}&\mid v\in\mathcal{S}\}\cup\{U_{v}^{*}\mid v\in V\setminus\mathcal{S}\})\\
&=\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})\Big)\end{split}

(21)

On the right hand side, for the sake of simplicity, we use
U_{v}
to denote
\widetilde{U_{v}}
for
v\in\mathcal{S}
and
U_{v}^{*}
for
v\in V\setminus\mathcal{S}
. Here, the contraction between
\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})
and
U_{w}
happens along the mode corresponding to the edge between
w
and its parent, and the contraction between
U_{w}
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})
happens along the mode corresponding to the edge between
w
and its
i^{th}
child (where the children can be ordered arbitrarily). Then, redefining
U_{w}^{*}
as

\begin{split}U_{w}^{*}:=\argmin_{U_{w}}\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})\Big)-A\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(22)

clearly can only decrease the error achieved by
\{\widetilde{U_{v}}\mid v\in\mathcal{S}\}\cup\{U_{v}^{*}\mid v\in V\setminus\mathcal{S}\}
. Thus, it suffices to show that for fixed values of
\widetilde{U_{v}}
(for
v\neq w
) and
U_{v}^{*}
,
\widetilde{U_{w}}
is a
(1+O(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}))
-approximate solution to this multiple-response
\ell_{2}
regression problem with probability
1-O(\frac{\delta}{|V|})
.

First, we analyze the effect of applying the Countsketch matrices
S_{v}
for
v\in\mathcal{S}
:

Lemma 11
.

With probability
1-O(\frac{\delta}{|V|})
, for all tensors
U_{w}
of the appropriate dimensions,

\begin{split}&\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\\
&=\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}\Big)\Big)\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})\Big)-A\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(23)

where if
v_{i}
is the
i^{th}
child of
w
, then we write
M_{v_{i}}
as
M_{i}
for simplicity. Here, for simplicity, we also write
\widetilde{U_{v}}
as
U_{v}
for
v\in\mathcal{S}
, and we write
U_{v}^{*}
as
U_{v}
for
v\in V\setminus\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Note that when we contract a Countsketch matrix
S_{v}
with
A
and with
\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})
, this has the same effect as matricizing
A
and
\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})
along the appropriate modes and multiplying both by a matrix of the form
S_{v}\otimes I
(where
I
is an identity matrix of the appropriate dimensions). Thus, replacing
A
with
A_{v}
, and replacing
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})
with
M_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq\deg(w)-1
, is equivalent to matricizing and then multiplying by a sequence of matrices of the form
S_{v}\otimes I
where
v
ranges over
\mathcal{S}
.

Now, observe that for each
v\in\mathcal{S}
,
S_{v}
has the
(O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q^{3/2}t\deg(w)}),O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)}),2)
JL moment property. This is because by Lemma
6
, in order for a Countsketch matrix to have the

(O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q^{3/2}t\deg(w)}),O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)}),2)
JL moment property, it must have
\Omega(\frac{q^{3}t^{2}\deg(w)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{q\deg(w)}{\delta})
=

\Omega(\frac{q^{6}k^{2}\deg(w)^{3}}{\varepsilon^{4}\delta}\log^{2}(\frac{q}{\delta}))
rows — in particular,
S_{v}
has
O(\frac{q^{6}k^{2}d^{3}}{\varepsilon^{4}\delta}\log^{2}(\frac{q}{\delta}))
rows where
d
is the maximum degree of any vertex in
\mathcal{T}
, which suffices. In addition, by Lemma
2
, it follows that
S_{v}\otimes I
also has the
(O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q^{3/2}t\deg(w)}),O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)}),2)
JL moment property.
4
4

4

For convenience, we write this map as
S_{v}\otimes I
. However, this does not imply that
S_{v}
is only applied to the first few modes of
A
— the subset of modes to which
S_{v}
is being applied can depend on
v
.

Now, observe that if
v_{1}\in V_{i}
and
v_{2}\in V_{j}
for
i\neq j
, then
S_{v_{1}}\otimes I
and
S_{v_{2}}\otimes I
commute, since the subset of modes of
A
to which
S_{v_{1}}
is applied is disjoint from the subset of modes of
A
to which
S_{v_{2}}
is applied. The linear map used to obtain
A_{v}
from
A
and
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}
from
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})
can be written as
\prod_{v\in\mathcal{S}}(S_{v}\otimes I)
. By the prior discussion, this map can also be written as

\prod_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\Big(\prod_{v\in V_{i}}(S_{v}\otimes I)\Big)\prod_{v\in V_{above}\cap\mathcal{S}}(S_{v}\otimes I)

Now, by Lemma
3
, for
1\leq i\leq\deg(w)-1
,
\prod_{v\in V_{i}}(S_{v}\otimes I)
has the
(O(\frac{\varepsilon}{qt\deg(w)}),O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)}),2)
JL moment property, since
S_{v}\otimes I
has the
(O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q^{3/t}t\deg(w)}),O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)}),2)
JL moment property for each
v\in V_{i}
, and moreover
|V_{i}|\leq|\mathcal{S}|\leq|V|=O(q)
. Similarly,
\prod_{v\in V_{above}\cap\mathcal{S}}(S_{v}\otimes I)
has the
(O(\frac{\varepsilon}{qt\deg(w)}),O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)}),2)
JL moment property. Thus, each of
\prod_{v\in V_{1}}(S_{v}\otimes I),\ldots,\prod_{v\in V_{\deg(w)-1}}(S_{v}\otimes I),\prod_{v\in V_{above}\cap\mathcal{S}}(S_{v}\otimes I)
has the
(O(\frac{\varepsilon}{qt\deg(w)}),O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)}),2)
JL moment property.

Finally, by Lemma
5
, with probability at least
1-O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)})
,
\prod_{v\in V_{i}}(S_{v}\otimes I)
is a
(1\pm O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q\deg(w)}))
affine embedding for
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})
(matricized appropriately). Note here that we used the fact that the edge between
w
and its
i^{th}
child
v_{i}
has rank
t
, meaning that
\prod_{v\in V_{i}}(S_{v}\otimes I)
simply has to be an affine embedding for a subspace of dimension
t
. Similarly,
\prod_{v\in V_{above}\cap\mathcal{S}}(S_{v}\otimes I)
is a
(1\pm O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q\deg(w)}))
affine embedding for
\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})
with probability at least
1-O(\frac{\delta}{q\deg(w)})
. Thus, by a union bound, with probability at least
1-O(\frac{\delta}{q})
, for all
U_{w}
of appropriate dimensions,

\begin{split}&\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\\
&=\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{q\deg(w)}\Big)\Big)^{\deg(w)}\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})\Big)-A\Big\|_{F}\\
&=\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{q}\Big)\Big)\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})\Big)-A\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(24)

as desired (note that this implies the lemma since
|V|=\Theta(q)
).
∎

Next, we analyze the effect of applying the Countsketch matrix
T_{v}
to the modes of
A
corresponding to
\mathcal{T}_{above}
:

Lemma 12
.

With probability
1-O(\frac{\delta}{|V|})
, for all tensors
U_{w}
of the appropriate dimensions,

\begin{split}\Big\|T_{v}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{V}&\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-T_{v}\circ A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\\
&=\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}\Big)\Big)\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(25)

Proof.

After matricizing
\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})
and
A_{v}
appropriately, it is sufficient to show that
T_{v}
is a
(1\pm O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q}))
affine embedding for a
k
-dimensional subspace (and
A_{v}
, matricized appropriately) with probability at least
1-O(\frac{\delta}{q})
. However, since
T_{v}
has
O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})
rows, it has the
(O(\frac{\varepsilon}{qk}),O(\frac{\delta}{q}),2)
JL moment property by Lemma
6
. It thus follows from Lemma
5
that
T_{v}
is a
(1\pm O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q}))
-approximation for
\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})
and
A_{v}
, matricized appropriately.
∎

Finally, applying the dense sign matrix
R_{v}
leads to a
(1+O(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}))
-approximate solution to multiple-response
\ell_{2}
regression:

Lemma 13
.

Define

\begin{split}\widehat{U_{w}}&:=\argmin_{U_{w}}\Big\|R_{v}T_{v}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-R_{v}T_{v}\circ A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(26)

Then, with probability
1-O(\frac{\delta}{|V|})
,

\begin{split}\Big\|T_{v}\circ&\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ\widehat{U_{w}}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-T_{v}\circ A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\\
&\leq\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}\Big)\Big)\min_{U_{w}}\Big\|T_{v}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-T_{v}\circ A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(27)

Proof.

We wish to show that after contracting
R_{v}
with
T_{v}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})
and
T_{v}\circ A_{v}
, along the mode corresponding to the row dimension of
T_{v}
, that the minimizer of the regression problem with
R_{v}
is a
(1+O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q}))
-approximate solution to the regression problem without
R_{v}
. However, the regression problem

\begin{split}\min_{U_{w}}\Big\|T_{v}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-T_{v}\circ A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(28)

can be rewritten in the following form:

\begin{split}\min_{U_{w}}\Big\|U_{w}\circ\Big((T_{v}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)\Big)-T_{v}\circ A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(29)

Without loss of generality, assume
U_{w}
has one mode of dimension
n
, in which case
29
is a multiple-response
\ell_{2}
regression problem. Let
i_{w}
be the mode of
U_{w}
of dimension
n
— then, matricizing the above problem along mode
i_{w}
gives

\begin{split}\min_{U_{w}}\Big\|M_{i_{w}}(U_{w})\Big(M(T_{v}\circ(\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})))\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M(M_{i})\Big)\Big)-M_{i_{w}}(T_{v}\circ A_{v})\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(30)

Here,
M(T_{v}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))
and
M(M_{i})
denote the matricizations of
\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})
and
M_{i}
along the modes corresponding to their edges connecting them to
U_{w}
. By the definition of
T_{v}
,
M(T_{v}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))
is a
k\times O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})
matrix.

Now, it suffices to show that the conclusion of Theorem
14
holds for the problem

\begin{split}\min_{U_{w}}\Big\|M_{i_{w}}(U_{w})\Big(M(T_{w}\circ(\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}&\mid v\in V_{above}\})))\\
&\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M(M_{i})\Big)\Big)(R_{w}\otimes I)-M_{i_{w}}(T_{w}\circ A_{w})(R_{w}\otimes I)\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(31)

i.e. that the minimizer of
31
is a
(1+O(\frac{\varepsilon}{q}))
-approximate solution to
30
. By Theorem
14
, it suffices to show that
R_{w}\otimes I
has the
(O(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{qk}}),O(\frac{\delta}{q}))
approximate matrix product property, and that it is a
(1\pm\frac{1}{3})
subspace embedding for
\Big(M(T_{w}\circ(\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})))\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M(M_{i})\Big)\Big)
with probability at least
1-O(\frac{\delta}{q})
.

By Lemma
10
,
R_{w}
has the
(O(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{qk}}),O(\frac{\delta}{q}),O(\log(\frac{q}{\delta})))
JL moment property as long as it has
O(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon}\log(\frac{q}{\delta}))
rows, which it does — by Lemma
2
, this implies that
R_{w}\otimes I
also has the
(O(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{qk}}),O(\frac{\delta}{q}),O(\log(\frac{q}{\delta})))
JL moment property. Thus by Theorem
6
,
R_{w}\otimes I
has the
(O(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{qk}}),O(\frac{\delta}{q}))
approximate matrix product property. In addition, by Lemma
9
, it suffices for
R_{w}
to be a
(1\pm\frac{1}{3})
subspace embedding for
M(T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))
which is a rank
k
matrix — by Lemma
8
, this holds as long as
R_{w}
has
O(k\log(q/\delta))
rows. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Combining the previous three lemmas, we find that if

\begin{split}\widehat{U_{w}}&:=\argmin_{U_{w}}\Big\|R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}\Big)-R_{w}T_{w}\circ A_{v}\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(32)

then with probability
1-O(\frac{\delta}{|V|})
,

\begin{split}&\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ\widehat{U_{w}}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})\Big)-A\Big\|_{F}\\
&=\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}\Big)\Big)\min_{U_{w}}\Big\|\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})\circ U_{w}\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}\mathcal{T}_{i}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{i}\})\Big)-A\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(33)

However, the regression problem
32
can be rewritten as the Kronecker product regression problem

\begin{split}\widehat{U_{w}}:=\argmin_{U_{w}}\Big\|U_{w}\circ\Big(M(R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}&\mid v\in V_{above}\}))\\
&\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M(M_{i})\Big)\Big)-M(R_{w}T_{w}\circ A_{w})\Big\|_{F}\end{split}

(34)

Thus, the minimizer
\widehat{U_{w}}
can be obtained by multiplying
M(R_{w}T_{w}\circ A_{w})
on the right by the pseudo-inverse of the Kronecker product
\Big(M(R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M(M_{i})\Big)\Big)
, which in turn is the Kronecker product of the pseudo-inverse of the individual components. Thus,

\begin{split}\widehat{U_{w}}&=(R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\})^{\dagger}\circ(R_{w}T_{w}\circ A_{w})\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}^{\dagger}\Big)\end{split}

(35)

Observe that
M_{i}
and therefore
M_{i}^{\dagger}
is known.
U_{v}
is not known for all
v\in V_{above}
, meaning that
(R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))^{\dagger}
cannot be computed efficiently. However,
(R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))^{\dagger}
is a
t\times t
matrix where
t=O(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon}\log(\frac{q}{\delta}))
. Observe that, since we define

\widetilde{U_{w}}=(R_{w}T_{w}\circ A_{w})\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\deg(w)-1}M_{i}^{\dagger}\Big)

we have
\widehat{U_{w}}=(R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))^{\dagger}\circ\widetilde{U_{w}}
. Thus, instead of contracting
(R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))^{\dagger}
with
\widetilde{U_{w}}
, we can contract it with
U_{w^{\prime}}^{*}
, where
w^{\prime}
is the parent of
w
. In other words, we redefine
U_{w^{\prime}}^{*}
as
U_{w^{\prime}}^{*}\circ(R_{w}T_{w}\circ\mathcal{T}_{above}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V_{above}\}))^{\dagger}
, where the contraction occurs along the mode of
U_{w^{\prime}}^{*}
corresponding to the edge between
w^{\prime}
and
w
. This has the effect that the edge between
w
and
w^{\prime}
now has rank
t=O(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon}\log(\frac{q}{\delta}))
instead of
k
. In summary, if we define
\widetilde{U_{w}}
and redefine
U_{w^{\prime}}^{*}
as above, and append
w
to
\mathcal{S}
, then it still holds that

\|\mathcal{T}(\{\widetilde{U_{v}}\mid v\in S\}\cup\{U_{v}^{*}\mid v\in V\setminus\mathcal{S}\})-A\|_{F}\leq\Big(1+O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{|V|}\Big)\Big)^{|\mathcal{S}|}\|\mathcal{T}(\{U^{*}_{v}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}

with probability
1-O(\frac{\delta|\mathcal{S}|}{|V|})
, as desired. This completes the induction.

Finally, we calculate the running time of Algorithm
3
. First, we calculate the time required to process a leaf
v\in L
. The time needed to compute
\widetilde{U_{v}}=M_{i}(A)T_{v}^{T}R_{v}^{T}
is
\text{nnz}(A)+n\cdot O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})\cdot t
, which is
\text{nnz}(A)+n\cdot\text{poly}(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon})
. We then compute
M_{v}=S_{v}\widetilde{U_{v}}
, which takes
O(snt)=n\cdot\text{poly}(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon})
time. We then perform the update
A\leftarrow S_{v}\circ_{i}A
, which takes
\text{nnz}(A)
time — this is because over the course of the algorithm,
\text{nnz}(A_{v})
is at most
\text{nnz}(A)
, since
\text{nnz}(A_{v})
is obtained by contracting
A
with a series of Countsketch matrices. Since there are
|L|=O(q)
leaves, the total time required to process the leaves is
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot\text{poly}(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon})
.

Next, we calculate the time needed to process vertices
v\in V\setminus L
. If
v
is not the root of
T
, then
\widetilde{U_{v}}
is defined as
(M^{\dagger}_{v-subtree}\circ_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}A\circ_{G_{2}}(R_{v}T_{v})
. We can efficiently perform this computation in the following manner:

1.

Contract
A
with
T_{v}
, which takes
\text{nnz}(A)
time. After contracting
A
with
T_{v}
, we obtain a tensor with dimensions
s\times\ldots\times s\times O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})\times n
, i.e. it has dimension
s
on the
\deg(w)-1
modes corresponding to the children of
w
. (Without loss of generality, we are assuming that
w
has a single mode of dimension
n
.)

2.

Contract
A\circ T_{v}
with
R_{v}
, which takes time
s^{\deg(w)-1}\cdot O(\frac{q^{3}k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\delta})\cdot n\cdot t=n\cdot\text{poly}(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(\deg(w))}=n\cdot(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(d)}
.

3.

Contract
A\circ(R_{v}T_{v})
with
M_{v-subtree}^{\dagger}
. This can be done by separately contracting
A\circ(R_{v}T_{v})
with
M_{u}
for each child
u
of
v
. There are
O(d)
such contractions, and since
M_{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times t}
and
A\circ(R_{v}T_{v})
has dimension
s\times\ldots\times s\times t\times n
, each contraction takes
O(t\cdot s^{\deg(v)-1}\cdot t\cdot n)=n\cdot\text{poly}(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{\deg(v)}=n\cdot(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(d)}
time. Thus, this step overall takes
n\cdot(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(d)}
time.

Thus, for a single vertex
v\in V\setminus L
, computing
\widetilde{U_{v}}
takes
\text{nnz}(A)+n\cdot(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(d)}
time. Next, we compute
M_{v}
. This is done by contracting
M_{v-subtree}
with
\widetilde{U_{v}}
— this takes at most
n\cdot(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(d)}
time. Finally, contracting
A
with
S_{v}
takes
\text{nnz}(A)
time. Thus, processing a single vertex
v\in V\setminus L
takes
\text{nnz}(A)+n\cdot(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(d)}
time, and the overall running time of Algorithm
3
is
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot(\frac{qk}{\varepsilon\delta})^{O(d)}
.
∎

Appendix C
Tensors with Low General Tensor Network Rank

In this section, we consider the case when the
q
-mode tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
can be approximated well by a tensor network with rank
k
according to a graph
\mathcal{G}
. More specifically, suppose we are given a graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
with
|V|=q
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for a collection of tensors
\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\}
, such that for each
v\in V
,
U_{v}
has one mode of dimension
n
and
\deg(v)
modes of dimension
k
, we define
\mathcal{G}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})
to be the tensor obtained by contracting the
U_{v}
in the manner specified by the edges of
\mathcal{G}
. In this section, we give algorithms for approximating
A
by a tree network of small rank, which achieves error not much larger than
\min_{U_{v}}\|\mathcal{G}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}
.

C.1
Existence of a Binary Tree Network with Low Relative Error and Rank
k^{O(\deg(G)\text{tw}(G))}

First, we show the following existential result. Suppose
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
is a graph, and
\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\}
is a collection of tensors such that
U_{v}
has one mode of dimension
n
and
\deg(v)
modes of dimension
k
. Then, there exists a binary tree
\mathcal{T}=(V_{\mathcal{T}},E_{\mathcal{T}})
, and a collection of tensors
\{\widehat{U_{v}}\mid v\in V_{\mathcal{T}}\}
such that
\widehat{U_{v}}
has one mode of dimension
n
, as well as
\deg_{\mathcal{T}}(v)
modes of dimension
k^{t}
(where
t
is small as long as the maximum degree and treewidth of
\mathcal{G}
are small), such that

\mathcal{G}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})=\mathcal{T}(\{\widehat{U_{v}}\mid v\in V_{\mathcal{T}}\})

First, we formally define contraction of an edge in a graph:

Definition 18

(Edge Contraction)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph, and let
(u,v)\in E
. Then, contracting the edge
(u,v)
results in a new graph
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
, defined as follows.
V^{\prime}
is equal to
V
, but with
u
and
v
removed and replaced with a new vertex
w\not\in V
. In addition, all edges in
E
which are incident to
u
and
v
in the original graph
G
are now incident to
w
instead. (Note that if
G
has a vertex
z\neq u,v
such that
(z,u),(z,v)\in E
, then in the new graph
G^{\prime}
, we consider the edge
(z,w)
to occur twice.)

As part of the construction of the binary tree
\mathcal{T}
, we contract all of the edges of
\mathcal{G}
in sequence (and form new vertices which will be members of
\mathcal{T}
) until only one vertex of
\mathcal{G}
remains. In order to have
\mathcal{T}(\{\widehat{U_{v}}\mid v\in V_{\mathcal{T}}\})=\mathcal{G}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})
with a rank of at most
k^{t}
on all the edges of
\mathcal{T}
, it is enough for the degree of all the vertices of
G
to be at most
t
throughout the contraction process. The following result from
[
MS08
]
achieves this with
t=O(\deg(G)\text{tw}(G))
, where
\deg(G)
denotes the maximum degree of any vertex of
G
, and
\text{tw}(G)
denotes the treewidth of
G
.

Lemma 14

(Proposition 4.2 of
[
MS08
]
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. There exists an ordering
\pi
of
E
such that if the edges of
G
are contracted in the order specified by
\pi
, then the degree of any vertex in
G
at any point in the contraction process (including parallel edges, and counting self-loops as
1
edge) is at most
O(\deg(G)\text{tw}(G))
. Such an ordering can be computed in
|V|^{O(1)}e^{O(\deg(G)\text{tw}(G))}
time.

Proof.

By Proposition 4.2 of
[
MS08
]
, the maximum degree over the course of the contraction process is upper bounded by the treewidth
\text{tw}(G^{*})
of the line graph
G^{*}
of
G
, and such an ordering can be computed given a tree decomposition with this width. Furthermore, since
G^{*}
has at most
|V|^{2}
vertices, a tree decomposition of
G^{*}
with treewidth
O(\text{tw}(G^{*}))
can be computed in
|V|^{O(1)}e^{O(\text{tw}(G^{*}))}
time, by Theorem 4.3 of
[
MS08
]
. Finally, by Lemma 4.4 of
[
MS08
]
,
\text{tw}(G^{*})\leq\deg(G)(\text{tw}(G)+1)-1
, leading to the desired running time.
∎

Algorithm 4

Converting a general tensor network of rank
k
into a tree network of rank
k^{t}
given a contraction ordering with maximum degree
t

A graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
with a contraction ordering with maximum degree
t
,
\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\}
such that each
U_{v}
has one mode of dimension
n
and
\deg(v)
modes of dimension
k
.

A binary tree
\mathcal{T}=(V_{\mathcal{T}},E_{\mathcal{T}})
and
\{\widehat{U_{v}}\mid v\in V_{\mathcal{T}}\}
such that each
\widehat{U_{v}}
has at most one mode of dimension
n
, and each mode of
\widehat{U_{v}}
corresponding to an edge of
\mathcal{T}
has dimension at most
k^{t}

// Initialization

\pi\leftarrow
contraction ordering of
\mathcal{G}
with maximum degree
t

for

v\in V

do

V_{v}\leftarrow\{v\}

E_{v}\leftarrow\varnothing

\mathcal{T}_{v}\leftarrow(V_{v},E_{v})
(i.e. the singleton tree consisting of
v
)

end

for

// Performing edge contractions

for

e=(u,v)\in\pi

do

L_{1,u}\leftarrow
the modes of
U_{u}
which correspond to edges in
\mathcal{G}

L_{2,u}\leftarrow
the remaining modes of
U_{u}

U_{u},U_{u}^{\prime}\leftarrow
tensors such that

(1)
U_{u}\circ U_{u}^{\prime}
is equal to the original
U_{u}

(2) The edge connecting
U_{u}
and
U_{u}^{\prime}
is of rank
k^{t}

(3) The remaining modes of
U_{u}
correspond to the modes in
L_{1,u}

(4) The remaining modes of
U_{u}^{\prime}
correspond to the modes in
L_{2,u}

L_{1,v}\leftarrow
the modes of
U_{v}
which correspond to edges in
\mathcal{G}

L_{2,v}\leftarrow
the remaining modes of
U_{v}

U_{v},U_{v}^{\prime}\leftarrow
tensors such that

(1)
U_{v}\circ U_{v}^{\prime}
is equal to the original
U_{v}

(2) The edge connecting
U_{v}
and
U_{v}^{\prime}
is of rank
k^{t}

(3) The remaining modes of
U_{v}
correspond to the modes in
L_{1,v}

(4) The remaining modes of
U_{v}^{\prime}
correspond to the modes in
L_{2,v}

w\leftarrow
the vertex resulting from the contraction of
u
and
v

U_{w}\leftarrow U_{u}\circ U_{v}
where the contraction occurs along the edge
(u,v)

\mathcal{T}_{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{T}_{u}
with
u
replaced by
u^{\prime}

\mathcal{T}_{v}\leftarrow\mathcal{T}_{v}
with
v
replaced by
v^{\prime}

\mathcal{T}_{w}\leftarrow
the tree such that

(1)
w
is the root

(2) The children of
w
are
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}

(3) The subtrees of
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
are
\mathcal{T}_{u}
and
\mathcal{T}_{v}
respectively

end

for

w\leftarrow
the single vertex of
\mathcal{G}
resulting from the contraction

return

\mathcal{T}_{w}
,
\{U_{v}\mid v\text{ is a vertex of }\mathcal{T}_{w}\}

Theorem 18
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
be a graph, with corresponding factors
\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\}
. Then the tree network
\mathcal{T}_{w}(\{U_{v}\mid v\text{ is a vertex of }\mathcal{T}_{w}\})
returned by Algorithm
4
is equal to
\mathcal{G}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that at any point in Algorithm
4
, when contracting an edge
(u,v)
, it is possible to split
U_{v}
into two tensors
U_{v}
and
U_{v^{\prime}}
such that the edge connecting
U_{v}
and
U_{v^{\prime}}
has rank at most
k^{t}
. This follows from the fact that (excluding the vertices in
\mathcal{T}_{v}
)
v
has degree at most
t
within
\mathcal{G}
. Thus, the modes of
U_{v}
corresponding to the edges incident to
v
in
\mathcal{G}
together have dimension at most
k^{t}
(since the rank on any edge in
\mathcal{G}
is
k
). The desired result then follows from matricizing
U_{v}
with the row dimension corresponding to these modes.
∎

C.2
Approximating
A
with the Resulting Tree Network

We use the following procedure when contracting
\mathcal{G}
. At each point in this procedure, each vertex
u
in
\mathcal{G}
has a binary tree
\mathcal{T}_{u}
attached to it (
\mathcal{T}_{u}
is only connected to
\mathcal{G}
through
u
and initially consists only of
u
). When contracting an edge
(u,v)
, we create vertices
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
such that
u
is connected to
u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}
is connected to
T_{u}
(i.e.,
u
is no longer directly connected to the rest of
T_{u}
), and the same is true of
v
and
v^{\prime}
. If
U_{u}
has any modes of dimension
n
, then those modes will now be a part of the factor
U_{u^{\prime}}
corresponding to
u^{\prime}
, and all the modes that initially connected
U_{u}
to
\mathcal{T}_{u}
will now belong to
U_{u^{\prime}}
. This means that aside from the edge connecting
u
and
u^{\prime}
, the only modes of
U_{u}
are the ones corresponding to the edges between
u
and the rest of
\mathcal{G}
. Since
u
has degree at most
O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))
, this means that these modes together have dimension
k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))}
, meaning
U_{u}
has rank
k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))}
when matricized along these modes, and we can let the edge between
u
and
u^{\prime}
have rank
k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))}
. The final tree output by our algorithm will only consist of edges of the form
(u,u^{\prime})
obtained in this way, meaning that all of the edges in this tree will have rank
k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))}
. Thus, we can approximate
A
using our bicriteria algorithm for tree networks, with
\mathcal{T}
as the target tree and
k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))}
as the target rank.

Algorithm 5

Algorithm to approximate
A
using a binary tree network with ranks
k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))}
, achieving at most the error of a general network according to a graph
\mathcal{G}
(up to a
1+\varepsilon
factor). Note that in the second step of this algorithm, we apply Algorithm
4
— Algorithm
4
can simply use the graph
\mathcal{G}
together with the contraction order to output the binary tree
\mathcal{T}
, by ignoring the steps involving tensor contraction.

A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
, a graph
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
such that each vertex has one mode of dimension
n
, and each edge has dimension
k

A binary tree
\mathcal{T}
with
O(q)
vertices,
\{U_{v}\mid v\text{ is a vertex of }\mathcal{T}\}
such that
U_{v}
has at most one mode of dimension
n
and every other mode of
U_{v}
has dimension
O(\frac{qk^{\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G})}}{\varepsilon}\log(\frac{q}{\delta}))

\pi\leftarrow
contraction order of
\mathcal{G}
with maximum degree
O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))

\mathcal{T}\leftarrow
the tree that is output by Algorithm
4
given
\mathcal{G}
and
\pi

\{U_{v}\mid v\text{ is a vertex of }\mathcal{T}\}\leftarrow
the output of Algorithm
3
with
A
as the input tensor and
\mathcal{T}
as the tree, and target rank
k^{\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G})}

return

\mathcal{T}
,
\{U_{v}\mid v\text{ is a vertex of }\mathcal{T}\}

Theorem 19
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
be a
q
-mode tensor, and let
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
be a graph with
|V|=q
such that each vertex has one mode of dimension
n
, and the edges each have dimension
k
. Then, Algorithm
5
outputs a binary tree
\mathcal{T}
and factors
\{U_{v}\mid v\text{ is a vertex of }\mathcal{T}\}
such that

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{T}(\{U_{v}\mid v\text{ is a vertex of }\mathcal{T}\})-A\|_{F}&\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|\mathcal{G}(\{U_{v}\mid v\in V\})-A\|_{F}\end{split}

(36)

with probability at least
1-\delta
. The running time of Algorithm
5
is
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot\text{poly}(\frac{q}{\varepsilon\delta})k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))}
.

Proof.

The approximation guarantee follows directly from Theorem
18
and Theorem
17
. We now calculate the running time. By Lemma
14
, calculating the contraction order
\pi
can be done in
\text{poly}(q)e^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G})}
time. In addition, given the contraction ordering, the remaining steps of Algorithm
4
take
\text{poly}(q)
time. Finally, applying Algorithm
3
with
A
and
\mathcal{T}
as inputs, and target rank
k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G})}
, takes
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot\Big(\frac{qk^{\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G})}}{\varepsilon\delta}\Big)^{O(1)}
time (note that the maximum degree of
\mathcal{T}
is
O(1)
since it is a binary tree). Thus, the overall running time of Algorithm
5
is
O(q\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n\cdot\text{poly}(\frac{q}{\varepsilon\delta})k^{O(\deg(\mathcal{G})\text{tw}(\mathcal{G}))}
.
∎

Appendix D
Fixed Parameter Tractable (FPT) Algorithms

D.1
Preliminaries on Gaussian Matrices with Exactly
k
Rows

In this section, we first develop the properties of random matrices with exactly
k
rows and i.i.d.
N(0,1/k)
entries. We show that these can be used to obtain subspace embeddings and perform multiple-response
\ell_{2}
regression with
e^{-\text{poly}(k/\varepsilon)}
success probability — our algorithm for Tucker-
(p,q)
decomposition shown in the next subsection will instantiate these
\ell_{2}
regression sketches
e^{\text{poly}(k/\varepsilon)}
times to obtain a constant probability of success. We start by showing the subspace embedding property.

Lemma 15

(
e^{-\text{poly}(k/\varepsilon)}
Success Probability Subspace Embedding)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
have rank
k
, and let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}
have i.i.d.
N(0,1/k)
entries. Then, with probability at least
e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\frac{2}{3}\|Ax\|_{2}\leq\|SAx\|_{2}\leq\frac{2}{3}\|Ax\|_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be an orthonormal basis for the column span of
A
— then it suffices to show that with the desired probability, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\frac{2}{3}\|x\|_{2}\leq\|SUx\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{3}\|x\|_{2}
. First observe that by the rotational invariance of the Gaussian distribution,
SU\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
has i.i.d.
N(0,1/k)
entries. Let
M=SU
, and let
d>1
be an absolute constant. For
i\in[k]
and
j\in[k]
, note that the probability density function of
M_{ij}
is

f_{X}(x)=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}

For
i\in[k],j\in[k]
, note that the probability density function of
M_{ij}
is
f(x)=\sqrt{\frac{k}{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{kx^{2}}{2}}
. Thus, for fixed
i,j\in[k],i\neq j
, and a small constant
E<\frac{1}{2}
,

\mathbf{Pr}\Big[|M_{ij}|\leq\frac{E}{k^{d}}\Big]\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\cdot\frac{Ee^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{k^{d}}

This is true since for
x\in[-E/k^{d},E/k^{d}]
, because
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{k})
has
\mu=0
,
f(x)\geq f(E/k^{d})
. Expressing the cumulative density function with the Riemann integral, since the p.d.f is non-negative,

\mathbf{Pr}\Big[-E/k^{d}\leq M_{ij}\leq E/k^{d}\Big]\geq 2\cdot\frac{E}{k^{d}}\cdot f(E/k^{d})

and
e^{-\frac{kE^{2}}{2k^{2d-1}}}\geq e^{-1/2}
when
k,d\geq 1
and
E<1
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\Big[|M_{ij}|\leq\frac{E}{k^{d}}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\frac{k}{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{kx^{2}}{2}}\cdot\frac{2E}{k^{d}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\frac{k(E/k^{d})^{2}}{2}}\cdot\frac{E}{k^{d-\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\frac{E^{2}}{2k^{2d-1}}}\cdot\frac{E}{k^{d-\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{Ee^{-1/2}}{k^{d}}

The last inequality is true because
k^{d-\frac{1}{2}}\leq k^{d}
for
k,d\geq 1
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\Big[M_{ii}=1\pm\frac{E}{k^{d}}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq f(2)\cdot\frac{2E}{k^{d}}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\cdot e^{-2k}\cdot\frac{E}{k^{d}}

Now, let
\mathcal{E}
denote the event that
\|M-I\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{E}{k^{d}}
— here,
I\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is the identity matrix, and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
denotes the entrywise
\ell_{\infty}
norm of a matrix. Then, by the above,

\begin{split}\mathbf{Pr}\Big[\mathcal{E}\Big]&\geq\mathbf{Pr}\Big[|M_{ij}|\geq\frac{E}{k^{d}}\text{ for }i\neq j\text{ and }|M_{ii}|=1\pm\frac{E}{k^{d}}\text{ for }i\in[k]\Big]\\
&=\prod_{i\neq j}\mathbf{Pr}\Big[|M_{ij}|\geq\frac{E}{k^{d}}\Big]\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{Pr}\Big[|M_{ii}|=1\pm\frac{E}{k^{d}}\Big]\\
&\geq\prod_{i\neq j}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{Ee^{-1/2}}{k^{d}}\prod_{i\in[k]}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{Ee^{-2k}}{k^{d}}\\
&\geq\Omega\Big(\frac{1}{k^{d}}\Big)^{k^{2}}\cdot\Omega\Big(\frac{e^{-2k}}{k^{d}}\Big)^{k}\\
&\geq e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}\cdot e^{-\Theta(k^{2})}e^{-\Theta(k\log k)}\\
&\geq e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}\end{split}

(37)

Furthermore, note that if
\mathcal{E}
holds, then we can write
M=I+T
, where
\|T\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{E}{k^{d}}
, meaning that

\|T\|_{2}\leq\|T\|_{F}\leq k\|T\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{E}{k^{d-1}}

meaning that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

|\|SUx\|_{2}-\|x\|_{2}|\leq\|Tx\|_{2}\leq\frac{E}{k^{d-1}}\|x\|_{2}\leq E\|x\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{3}\|x\|_{2}

where the second-to-last inequality holds for
d\geq 1
, and the last inequality holds for
E\leq\frac{1}{3}
. In summary, with probability at least
e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\frac{2}{3}\|Ax\|_{2}\leq\|SAx\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{3}\|Ax\|_{2}

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Next, we show that
S\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}
with i.i.d.
N(0,1/k)
entries also satisfies a special case of the approximate matrix product property in Definition
12
, where the success probability is now
e^{-\text{poly}(k/\varepsilon)}
instead of
1-\delta
.

Lemma 16

(
e^{-\text{poly}(kt/\varepsilon)}
Success Probability Approximate Matrix Product)
.

Let
n,k,t\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
, and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times t}
. Assume that for
i,j\in[k]
distinct,
\langle A_{i},A_{j}\rangle=0
, and for
i,j\in[t]
distinct,
\langle B_{i},B_{j}\rangle=0
. Further assume that for all
i\in[k]
and
j\in[t]
,
\langle A_{i},B_{j}\rangle=0
. Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}
have i.i.d.
N(0,1/k)
entries. Then, with probability
e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}\cdot(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})^{O(kt)}
, the following hold:

•

\|A^{T}S^{T}SB\|_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\|A\|_{F}^{2}\|B\|_{F}^{2}
, and

•

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\frac{2}{3}\|Ax\|_{2}\leq\|SAx\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{3}\|Ax\|_{2}
.

Remark 7
.

Note that the second property is the conclusion of Lemma
15
— here we restate it to clarify that the approximate matrix product and the subspace embedding property hold simultaneously (note that we cannot union bound to show this, as in usual arguments, since the success probabilities are exponentially small).

Proof.

Observe that if we let
M=[A,B]
, then the columns of
M
are orthogonal, meaning the entries of
SM
are jointly independent. In particular, let
\mathcal{E}
be the event that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\frac{2}{3}\|Ax\|_{2}\leq\|SAx\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{3}\|Ax\|_{2}
— by Lemma
15
, this holds with probability
e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}
. Since
SA
and
SB
are independent, if we condition on
\mathcal{E}
, the entries of
SB
are jointly independent.

Now, it suffices to show that, for each
i\in[k]
and
j\in[t]
, if we condition on
\mathcal{E}
, then with probability
e^{-\text{poly}(kt/\varepsilon)}
,
|A_{i}^{T}S^{T}SB_{j}|^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\|A_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\|B_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
. Note that if
\mathcal{E}
holds, then
\|SA_{i}\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{3}\|A_{i}\|_{2}
, meaning that

|A_{i}^{T}S^{T}SB_{j}|^{2}\leq\|SA_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\|SB_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq O(1)\|A_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\|SB_{j}\|_{2}^{2}

where the first inequality is by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the second is by conditioning on
\mathcal{E}
.

Let us now analyze the probability that
\|SB_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\Theta(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})\|B_{j}\|_{2}^{2}
. Since the Gaussian distribution is
2
-stable,
SB_{j}
is a random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
N(0,\frac{\|B_{j}\|_{2}^{2}}{k})
entries. For convenience, let
X=SB_{j}
. Then, for
i\in[k]
,
Y_{i}=\frac{X_{i}}{\|B_{i}\|_{2}/\sqrt{k}}
is drawn from
N(0,1)
, meaning

\mathbf{Pr}\Big[|X_{i}|\leq\Theta\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{k}\Big)\|B_{j}\|_{2}\Big]\geq\mathbf{Pr}\Big[|Y_{i}|\leq\Theta\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{k}}\Big)\Big]\geq\Theta\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{k}}\Big)

Thus,

\mathbf{Pr}\Big[|X_{i}|\leq\Theta\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{k}\Big)\|B_{j}\|_{2}\text{ for all }i\in[k]\Big]\geq\Theta\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{k}}\Big)^{k}\geq\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\Big)^{O(k)}

Let
\mathcal{E}_{j}
denote the event that each coordinate of
SB_{j}
is at most
O(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{k})\|B_{j}\|_{2}
in absolute value — we have shown that
\mathbf{Pr}[\mathcal{E}_{j}]\geq(\varepsilon/k)^{O(k)}
. Note that if
\mathcal{E}_{j}
holds, then

\|SB_{j}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{k}\|SB_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{k}\cdot O\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{k}\Big)\|B_{j}\|_{2}\leq O\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{k}}\Big)\|B_{j}\|_{2}

In summary, if
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}_{j}
hold, then for all
i\in[k]
,

|A_{i}^{T}S^{T}SB_{j}|^{2}\leq O(1)\|A_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\|SB_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\Big)\|A_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\|B_{j}\|_{2}^{2}

meaning that if
\mathcal{E}
holds and
\mathcal{E}_{j}
holds for all
j\in[t]
, then

\|A^{T}S^{T}SB\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{j\in[t]}|A_{i}^{T}S^{T}SB_{j}|^{2}\leq\sum_{i\in[k]}\sum_{j\in[t]}O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\Big)\|A_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\|B_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq O\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\Big)\|A\|_{F}^{2}\|B\|_{F}^{2}

The probability that
\mathcal{E}
holds and
\mathcal{E}_{j}
holds for all
j\in[t]
is

\mathbf{Pr}\Big[\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\mathcal{E}_{t}\Big]=\mathbf{Pr}\Big[\mathcal{E}\Big]\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathbf{Pr}\Big[\mathcal{E}_{i}\Big]\geq e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\Big)^{O(k)}=e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}\cdot\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\Big)^{O(kt)}

since the entries of
SB
are jointly independent when conditioning on
\mathcal{E}
. This proves the lemma.
∎

Using the above two lemmas, we show that random matrices with exactly
k
rows and i.i.d.
N(0,1/k)
entries can be used for multiple-response
\ell_{2}
regression with success probability
e^{-\text{poly}(k/\varepsilon)}
, following the proof of Theorem 3.1 in
[
CW09
]
.

Lemma 17

(
e^{-\text{poly}(kt/\varepsilon)}
Success Probability for Multiple-Response
\ell_{2}
Regression)
.

Let
n,k,s,t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
k,t<n
. Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
has rank
k
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}
has rank
t
. Let
R\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}
have i.i.d.
N(0,1/k)
entries. If
X^{*}=\argmin\|AX-B\|_{F}^{2}
and
\widehat{X}=\argmin\|RAX-RB\|_{F}^{2}
, then
\|A\widehat{X}-B\|_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}
with probability at least
e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})^{O(kt)}
.

Proof.

The proof follows that of Theorem 3.1 in
[
CW09
]
. By the normal equations and the Pythagorean theorem,

\|A\widehat{X}-B\|_{F}^{2}=\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})+AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}=\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}^{2}+\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}

since the columns of
AX^{*}-B
are orthogonal to those of
A
. Now we bound
\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}
as in the proof of Lemma 3.3 of
[
CW09
]
. Let
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be an orthonormal basis for the column span of
A
. Condition on the event
\mathcal{E}
, that
\|U^{T}R^{T}R(AX^{*}-B)\|_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\|U\|_{F}^{2}\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}=\varepsilon\|AX^{*}-B\|_{F}^{2}
and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\frac{2}{3}\|x\|_{2}\leq\|RUx\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{3}\|x\|_{2}
— by Lemma
16
,
\mathcal{E}
occurs with probability at least
e^{-\Theta(k^{2}\log k)}e^{-k(k+t)}
. Then,

\begin{split}\|A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}&=\|UU^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\|U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\|(U^{T}R^{T}RU)U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})+(I-U^{T}R^{T}RU)U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\|U^{T}R^{T}RUU^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\|(I-U^{T}R^{T}RU)U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&=\|U^{T}R^{T}RA(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\|I-U^{T}R^{T}RU\|_{2}\|U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\|U^{T}R^{T}RA(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}+\frac{1}{3}\|U^{T}A(\widehat{X}-X^{*})\|_{F}\\
