\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\widehat{U_{1}})_{i}\otimes\cdots(\widehat{U_{q}})_{i}-A\rVert_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{OPT}

Proof.

The theorem immediate follows Lemma
21
and
22
, and that
\min_{X_{1},\cdots,X_{q}}\lVert X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{q}-A\rVert_{F}^{2}
could be solved as a polynomial system in
2^{\Omega(k^{q})}
.
∎

Appendix E
Hardness of Rank-
1
Decomposition with Frobenius Norm

Definition 22
.

Let
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. Then, we define
\|B\|_{2,4}=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|B_{i,:}\|_{2}^{4}\Big)^{1/4}
, where
B_{i,:}
is the
i^{th}
row of
B
, and
\|B\|_{2\to 4}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|Bx\|_{4}}{\|x\|_{2}}
.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Let
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and define
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}
as follows: for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
A^{k}=B_{k}B_{k}^{T}
, where
B_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the transpose of the
k^{th}
row of
B
. Then, for any unit vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\begin{split}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T}-A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}&=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T}\|_{F}^{2}-2\Tr((A^{k})^{T}uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T})+\|A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T}\|_{F}^{2}-2\Tr(A^{k}uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T})+\|A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(u^{T}A^{k}v)^{2}-2\Tr(A^{k}uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T})+\|A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(u^{T}A^{k}v)^{2}-2\Tr(u^{T}A^{k}vv^{T}A^{k}u)+\|A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}-(u^{T}A^{k}v)^{2}\end{split}

(76)

The third equality above is because
u
and
v
are unit vectors. The fourth equality above is because
\Tr(XYZ)=\Tr(ZXY)
for any matrices
X,Y,Z
. The fifth equality is because
A^{k}
is PSD (meaning that
v^{T}A^{k}u=u^{T}A^{k}v
). To simplify the final expression in Equation
76
, we use the following Lemma:

Lemma 23

(From
[
HLZ10
]
, Proposition 6.10 of
[
HL13
]
)
.

Let
A_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a symmetric matrix for
k\in[m]
. Then,

\max_{\|u\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\langle A_{k}u,u\rangle^{2}=\max_{\|x\|_{2}=\|y\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\langle A_{k}x,y\rangle^{2}

Proof.

This result is shown in the proof of Proposition 2 of
[
HLZ10
]
.
∎

Thus, by Equation
76
,

\begin{split}\min_{\|u\|_{2}=\|v\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T}-A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}&=\min_{\|u\|_{2}=\|v\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|B_{k}\|_{2}^{4}-(u^{T}A^{k}v)^{2}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|B_{k}\|_{2}^{4}-\max_{\|u\|_{2}=\|v\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(u^{T}A^{k}v)^{2}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|B_{k}\|_{2}^{4}-\max_{\|u\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(u^{T}A^{k}u)^{2}\\
&=\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\max_{\|u\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(u^{T}B_{k}B_{k}^{T}u)^{2}\\
&=\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\max_{\|u\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle u,B_{k}\rangle^{2}\langle B_{k},u\rangle^{2}\\
&=\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\max_{\|u\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle B_{k},u\rangle^{4}\\
&=\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\max_{\|u\|_{2}=1}\|Bu\|_{4}^{4}\\
&=\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}\end{split}

(77)

where the third equality is by Lemma
23
, since
A^{k}
is a PSD matrix.

Finally, we will show that obtaining a
(1+\Theta(\frac{1}{n}))
-approximate solution
u,v
to Tucker-
(2,3)
decomposition would allow us to compute a
\Theta(1)
-approximation to
\|B\|_{2\to 4}
. To show this, we compare
\|B\|_{2,4}
to
\|B\|_{2\to 4}
:

Lemma 24
.

\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}\geq\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}\geq\frac{\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}}{n}
.

Proof.

First, for any
k\in[n]
, let
B_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the transpose of the
k^{th}
row of
B
. Then,

\begin{split}\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}&\geq\Big\|B\cdot\frac{B_{k}}{\|B_{k}\|_{2}}\Big\|_{4}^{4}\\
&\geq\frac{\langle B_{k},B_{k}\rangle^{4}}{\|B_{k}\|_{2}^{4}}\\
&=\|B_{k}\|_{2}^{4}\end{split}

(78)

Thus,

\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}\geq\max_{k\in[n]}\|B_{k}\|_{2}^{4}\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|B_{k}\|_{2}^{4}=\frac{\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}}{n}

This shows the second inequality in the lemma. The first equality follows from Equation
77
.
∎

Now suppose there is an algorithm which computes unit vectors
u,v
such that

\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T}-A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\Big(1+\frac{1}{Cn}\Big)\min_{\|u_{*}\|_{2}=\|v_{*}\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|u_{*}u_{*}^{T}A^{k}v_{*}v_{*}^{T}-A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}

for some absolute constant
C\geq 1
. Then, this algorithm computes
F
such that

\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}\leq F\leq\Big(1+\frac{1}{Cn}\Big)\cdot(\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4})

It follows from the left inequality that

\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-F\leq\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}

and from the right inequality that

\begin{split}\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-F&\geq\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\Big(1+\frac{1}{Cn}\Big)\cdot(\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4})\\
&=\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}-\frac{1}{Cn}\cdot(\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4})\\
&=\Big(1+\frac{1}{Cn}\Big)\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}-\frac{\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}}{Cn}\\
&\geq\Big(1+\frac{1}{Cn}\Big)\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}-\frac{\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}}{C}\\
&\geq\Big(1-\frac{1}{C}+\frac{1}{Cn}\Big)\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}\end{split}

(79)

where the second inequality is by Lemma
24
. In summary,

\Big(1-\frac{1}{C}+\frac{1}{Cn}\Big)\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}\leq\|B\|_{2,4}^{4}-F\leq\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}

Thus,
\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}-F
is equal to
\|B\|_{2\to 4}^{4}
up to a constant factor, and assuming Conjecture
1
, it thus requires at least
2^{\Omega(n)}
time to compute unit vectors
u,v
satisfying

\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|uu^{T}A^{k}vv^{T}-A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\Big(1+\frac{1}{Cn}\Big)\min_{\|u_{*}\|_{2}=\|v_{*}\|_{2}=1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|u_{*}u_{*}^{T}A^{k}v_{*}v_{*}^{T}-A^{k}\|_{F}^{2}

In particular, finding a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation for Tucker-
(2,3)
, for rank
k=1
, requires at least
2^{\Omega(1/\varepsilon)}
time assuming Conjecture
1
.
∎

Appendix F
Tucker-
(p,q)
Decomposition with A Robust Loss Function

In this section, we give an algorithm for Tucker-
(p,q)
decomposition, using the robust loss function
\|\cdot\|_{R}
, defined in Definition
9
. Formally, we solve the following problem:

Problem 5

(Robust Tucker-
(p,q)
Decomposition)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
be a
q
-mode tensor, with
p<q
, and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, we wish to find a
q
-mode tensor
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
of multilinear rank at most
(k,\ldots,k,n,\ldots,n)
(where the first
p
entries of the tuple are
k
and the last
q-p
entries are
n
), such that
\|X-A\|_{R}
is minimized.

For convenience, we will use the following notation in this section:

Definition 23
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
be a
q
-mode tensor. Then, we define
T^{i}:=T(:,\ldots,:,i)
, that is,
T^{i}
is the slice of
T
whose index in the last mode is
i
.

Algorithm 10

Algorithm for robust Tucker-
(p,q)
decomposition with bicriteria rank
O(k\log^{2}k)
on each mode. Here
\textsc{12NormApprox}(A,k)
denotes an algorithm for finding the best (up to an
O(1)
factor) rank-
k
approximation to
A
in the
\ell_{1,2}
norm, with bicriteria rank
O(k\log^{2}k)
.

A
q
-mode tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
,
p<q
,
k\in\mathbb{N}

U^{1},\ldots,U^{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times O(k\log^{2}k)}

for

m=1\to p

do

for

i=1\to n

do

S_{m,i}\leftarrow
An
O(pnk^{2}\log^{4}k)\times n^{q-1}
Countsketch matrix

T_{m,i}\leftarrow
An
O(k\log^{2}k+\log(np))\times O(pnk^{2}\log^{4}k)
i.i.d. sign matrix

B_{m,i}\leftarrow M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times O(k\log^{2}k+\log(np))}

end

for

B_{m}\leftarrow[B_{m,1},\ldots,B_{m,n}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\cdot O(k\log^{2}k+\log(np))}

U,V\leftarrow\textsc{12NormApprox}(B_{m},k)
(best result after
O(\log p)
trials)

\widehat{U^{m}}\leftarrow U

end

for

return

\widehat{U^{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U^{p}}

Theorem 21
.

There is an algorithm for Problem
5
(Algorithm
10
) which, given a
q
-mode tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, outputs
\widehat{U^{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U^{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times O(k\log^{2}k)}
for which, with probability at least
\frac{4}{5}
,

\begin{split}\min_{G}\Big\|A-\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}}G(i_{1},\ldots,i_{p},i_{p+1},&\ldots,i_{q})\widehat{U^{1}}(:,i_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\widehat{U^{p}}(:,i_{p})\otimes e_{i_{p+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e_{i_{q}}\Big\|_{R}\\
&\leq O(p\cdot(\sqrt{k}\log k+\sqrt{\log(np)}))\cdot\min_{X\text{ rank }(k,\ldots,k,n,\ldots,n)}\|X-A\|_{R}\end{split}

(80)

The running time of Algorithm
10
is
O(p\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n^{3}\log n\cdot\text{poly}(k)
.

Proof.

Define

\mathcal{OPT}=\min_{X\text{ rank }(k,\ldots,k,n\ldots,n)}\|X-A\|_{R}

Let
X
be a tensor of multilinear rank at most
(k,\ldots,k,n,\ldots,n)
. Then, we can write

X=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{p}\in[p],i_{p+1},\ldots,i_{q}\in[n]}G(i_{1},\ldots,i_{p},i_{p+1},\ldots,i_{q})U^{1}(:,i_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes U^{p}(:,i_{p})\otimes e_{i_{p+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e_{i_{q}}

for some
U^{1},\ldots,U^{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, where for
j\in[n]
,
e_{j}
is the
j^{th}
standard basis vector. Thus, for any
i\in[q]
, we can write

\begin{split}X^{i}&=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}\in[p],i_{p+1},\ldots,i_{q-1}\in[n]}G(i_{1},\ldots,i_{p},i_{p+1},\ldots,i_{q-1},i)U^{1}(:,i_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes U^{p}(:,i_{p})\otimes e_{i_{p+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e_{i_{q-1}}\\
&=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}\in[p],i_{p+1},\ldots,i_{q-1}\in[n]}G^{i}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{p},i_{p+1},\ldots,i_{q-1})U^{1}(:,i_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes U^{p}(:,i_{p})\otimes e_{i_{p+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e_{i_{q-1}}\end{split}

(81)

In particular, reshaping gives

\|X^{i}-A^{i}\|_{F}=\|(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}

for each
i\in[n]
, meaning that

\|X-A\|_{R}=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}

Now, observe that we can assume, without loss of generality, that
U^{m}
has orthonormal columns, for
m\in[p]
— otherwise, we can simply modify
G^{i}
appropriately for each
i\in[n]
, since

\begin{split}\|X-A\|_{R}&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|U^{m}M_{m}(G^{i})(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{m-1}\times U^{m+1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})^{T}-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}\end{split}

(82)

meaning we can multiply
M_{m}(G^{i})
on the left by an appropriate
k\times k
matrix. Next observe the following:

Lemma 25
.

Problem
5
is equivalent to

\min_{P^{1},\ldots,P^{p},G^{1},\ldots,G^{n}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}

where for
m\in[p]
,
P^{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a rank-
k
orthogonal projection matrix, and for
i\in[n]
,
G^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
is a
(q-1)
-mode tensor.

Proof.

As shown above, Problem
5
is equivalent to

\min_{U^{1},\ldots,U^{p},G^{1},\ldots,G^{n}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}

Fix
U^{1},\ldots,U^{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, and
G^{1},\ldots,G^{n}
with the appropriate dimensions. Then, by reshaping, we find that

\begin{split}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}&\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|U^{1}M_{1}(G^{i})(U^{2}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})^{T}-M_{1}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|U^{1}(U^{1})^{T}M_{1}(\widetilde{G^{i}})(U^{2}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})^{T}-M_{1}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|P^{1}M_{1}(\widetilde{G^{i}})(U^{2}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})^{T}-M_{1}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times U^{2}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(\widetilde{G^{i}})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\end{split}

(83)

Here the first equality is by reshaping. For the second equality, first recall our assumption (without loss of generality) that
U^{1}
has orthonormal columns. We can also assume without loss of generality that the columns of
M_{1}(G^{i})
are in the column span of
(U^{1})^{T}
(otherwise, the portion of those columns that is in the null space of
U^{1}
could simply be discarded with no effect). Thus, there exists
\widetilde{G^{i}}
such that
M_{1}(G^{i})=(U^{1})^{T}M_{1}(\widetilde{G^{i}})
. Note that the dimensions of
\widetilde{G^{i}}
are the same as those of
G^{i}
, except that the dimension of
\widetilde{G^{i}}
in the first mode is
n
instead of
k
. For the third equality, we let
P^{1}=U^{1}(U^{1})^{T}
, which is an orthogonal projection matrix since
U^{1}
has orthonormal columns, and the fourth equality is by reshaping again.

In this way, we can repeat this argument for each mode
m\in[p]
to find that

\begin{split}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}&\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times P^{2}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(\widetilde{G^{i}})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\end{split}

(84)

where for
m\in[p]
,
P^{m}=U^{m}(U^{m})^{T}
. Therefore,

\begin{split}\min_{U^{m},G^{i}}\sum_{i\in[n]}&\|(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\\
&\geq\min_{P^{m},G^{i}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times P^{2}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(\widetilde{G^{i}})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\end{split}

(85)

The reverse inequality can also be obtained by reversing the argument in Equation
83
, letting
U^{1}
be an orthonormal basis for the column span of
P^{1}
. This proves the lemma.
∎

We now extend Lemma
25
slightly:

Lemma 26
.

Problem
5
is equivalent to

\begin{split}\min_{P^{1},\ldots,P^{p}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\end{split}

(86)

where for
m\in[p]
,
P^{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a rank-
k
orthogonal projection matrix, and for
i\in[n]
,
G^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\ldots\times n}
is a
(q-1)
-mode tensor.

Proof.

Note that Lemma
25
states that Problem
5
is equivalent to

\min_{P^{1},\ldots,P^{p},G^{1},\ldots,G^{n}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}

where the dimension of
G^{i}
is
n
in every mode. We now show that
G^{i}
can be replaced with
A^{i}
. For each
m\in[p]
, write
P^{m}=U^{m}(U^{m})^{T}
, where
U^{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
is an orthonormal basis for the column span of
P^{m}
. Then, by standard properties of the Kronecker product (see page 4 of
[
SDLF+17
]
),

\begin{split}P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}&\times\ldots\times I_{n}=(U^{1}(U^{1})^{T})\times\ldots\times(U^{p}(U^{p})^{T})\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n}\\
&=(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})((U^{1})^{T}\times\ldots\times(U^{p})^{T}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\\
&=(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})(U^{1}\times\ldots\times U_{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})^{T}\end{split}

(87)

Here the second equation is by the fact that for matrices
A,B,C,D
whose dimensions match appropriately,
(A\times B)(C\times D)=(AC\times BD)
, and the third equation is by the fact that
A^{T}\times B^{T}=(A\times B)^{T}
. Now, we recall a result from
[
DJS+19
]
:

Lemma 27

(Proposition 3.2 of
[
DJS+19
]
)
.

Let
U_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d_{i}}
be an orthonormal basis for
A_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d_{i}}
, for
i\in[q]
. Then,
U_{1}\times\ldots\times U_{q}
is an orthonormal basis for
A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{q}
.

In particular, it follows from Proposition 3.2 of
[
DJS+19
]
that
U^{1}\times\ldots\times U^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n}
has orthonormal columns, and therefore, by Equation
87
,
P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n}
is a projection matrix, meaning that the regression objective below:

\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(G^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}

is minimized when
G^{i}=A^{i}
, from which the lemma follows.
∎

Observe that from the proof of the above lemma, it is also clear that
P^{i}
corresponds to
U^{i}(U^{i})^{T}
in the first formulation of the problem, assuming that
U^{i}
has orthonormal columns. Now, using the above lemma, we show a lower bound on
\mathcal{OPT}
that is useful for bounding the approximation factor of Algorithm
10
:

Lemma 28
.

For all
m\in[p]
,

\mathcal{OPT}\geq\min_{P^{m}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}

where the minima are taken over rank-
k
orthogonal projection matrices
P^{m}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
26
,

\mathcal{OPT}=\min_{P^{1},\ldots,P^{p}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}

Fix rank-
k
orthogonal projection matrices
P^{1}_{*},\ldots,P^{p}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that

\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}_{*}\times\ldots\times P^{p}_{*}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}=\mathcal{OPT}

Then, for any
m\in[p]
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{OPT}&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}_{*}\times\ldots\times P^{p}_{*}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{2}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\|P^{m}_{*}M_{m}(A^{i})(P^{1}_{*}\times\ldots\times P^{m-1}_{*}\times P^{m+1}_{*}\times\ldots\times P^{p}_{*}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})^{T}-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&\geq\sum_{i\in[n]}\|P^{m}_{*}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}\end{split}

(88)

Here the second equality is by reshaping, and the first inequality is by the Pythagorean theorem (since
P^{m}_{*}M_{m}(A^{i})
is the closest matrix to
M_{m}(A^{i})
, in Frobenius norm, whose columns are in the column span of
P^{m}_{*}
). This proves the lemma.
∎

We next prove an auxiliary lemma that helps us upper bound the error of Algorithm
10
:

Lemma 29
.

Let
P^{1},\ldots,P^{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be orthogonal projection matrices. Then,

\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{F}\leq\sum_{m\in[p]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}

for any
i\in[n]
.

Proof.

Let us show by induction on
t\in[p]
that

\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{t}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{F}\leq\sum_{m\in[t]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}

from which the lemma follows. For the base case, observe that

\begin{split}\|(P^{1}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{F}&=\|P^{1}M_{1}(A^{i})-M_{1}(A^{i})\|_{F}\end{split}

(89)

by reshaping, as desired. Now, suppose for some
t\in[p]
, with
t<p
, that

\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{t}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{F}\leq\sum_{m\in[t]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}

Then,

\begin{split}\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{t+1}\times&I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&=\|P^{t+1}M_{t+1}(A^{i})(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{t}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})^{T}-M_{t+1}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\|P^{t+1}M_{t+1}(A^{i})(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{t}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})^{T}-P^{t+1}M_{t+1}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\|P^{t+1}M_{t+1}(A^{i})-M_{t+1}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\sum_{m\in[t+1]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}\end{split}

(90)

Here the first equality is by reshaping along the
(t+1)^{th}
mode. The first inequality is by the triangle inequality. The second inequality is by the induction hypothesis. This completes the induction, and thus the proof of the lemma.
∎

Finally, we perform a union bound over
i\in[n]
to show that
S_{m,i}^{T}
and
T_{m,i}^{T}
give
(\varepsilon,0,O(k\log^{2}k))
-PCPs for their respective
M_{m}(A^{i})
, with at least constant probability. (Here we let
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{10}
for convenience.)

Lemma 30
.

For
m\in[p]
and
i\in[n]
, let
\mathcal{E}_{m,i}
be the event that
S_{m,i}^{T}
is an
(\varepsilon,0,O(k\log^{2}k))
-PCP for
M_{m}(A^{i})
, and
\mathcal{F}_{m,i}
the event that
T_{m,i}^{T}
is an
(\varepsilon,0,O(k\log^{2}k))
-PCP for
M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}
. Finally, define
\mathcal{E}=\bigcap_{m\in[p],i\in[n]}(\mathcal{E}_{m,i}\cap\mathcal{F}_{m,i})
. Then,
\mathcal{E}
occurs with probability at least
\frac{9}{10}
, as long as
S_{m,i}
has at least
O(\frac{pnk^{2}\log^{4}k}{\varepsilon^{2}})
rows and
T_{m,i}
has at least
O(\frac{k\log^{2}k+\log(np)}{\varepsilon^{2}})
rows.

In addition, let
\mathcal{G}
be the event that, for all
m\in[p]
,

\min_{V}\|\widehat{U^{m}}V-B_{m}\|_{1,2}\leq O(1)\min_{X\text{ rank }k}\|X-B_{m}\|_{1,2}

where
\widehat{U^{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U^{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times O(k\log^{2}k)}
are the factors output by Algorithm
10
. Then,
\mathcal{G}
occurs with probability at least
\frac{9}{10}
.

Proof.

Suppose
S_{m,i}
has at least
O(\frac{pnk^{2}\log^{4}k}{\varepsilon^{2}})
rows. Then, by Theorem
10
with
\delta
replaced by
\frac{1}{20np}
,
\varepsilon
replaced by
\varepsilon
, and
k
replaced by
O(k\log^{2}k)
,
M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}
is an
(\varepsilon,0,O(k\log^{2}k))
-PCP for
M_{m}(A^{i})
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{20np}
. Thus, for each
m\in[p]
and
i\in[n]
,
\mathcal{E}_{m,i}
holds with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{20np}
. In addition, suppose
T_{m,i}
has at least
O(\frac{k\log^{2}k+\log(np)}{\varepsilon^{2}})
rows. Then, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{20np}
,
M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}
is an
(\varepsilon,0,O(k\log^{2}k))
-PCP for
M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}
. In summary,
\mathcal{E}_{m,i}
and
\mathcal{F}_{m,i}
hold with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{20np}
for all
m\in[p]
and
i\in[n]
, and by a union bound,
\mathcal{E}
holds with probability at least
\frac{9}{10}
.

In addition, note that for any
m\in[p]
, with probability
1-\frac{1}{10p}
,
\min_{V}\|U^{m}V-B_{m}\|_{1,2}\leq O(1)\min_{X\text{ rank }k}\|X-B_{m}\|_{1,2}
, since to obtain
U^{m}
, we apply the subroutine
12NormApprox
for
O(\log p)
trials and let
U^{m}
be result of the trial which gives the least
\ell_{1,2}
norm error. Thus, by a union bound,
\mathcal{G}
occurs with probability at least
\frac{9}{10}
.
∎

Now, we analyze the error of Algorithm
10
. Let
\widehat{U^{1}},\ldots,\widehat{U^{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times O(k\log^{2}k)}
be the output of Algorithm
10
, and let
\widehat{P^{1}},\ldots,\widehat{P^{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be the respective orthogonal projection matrices (i.e.
\widehat{P^{m}}
is the orthogonal projection onto the column span of
\widehat{U^{m}}
). In addition, condition on
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{G}
(which occur simultaneously with probability at least
\frac{4}{5}
by Lemma
30
). Then, the error obtained by Algorithm
10
can be bounded as follows. In the following sequence of inequalities, we use
a\lesssim b
to denote
a\leq Cb
for some absolute constant
C
that is independent of
a
and
b
.

\begin{split}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}&\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&\leq\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}-M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}\|_{F}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}-M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}\|_{F}\\
&\leq\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|P^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}-M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}\|_{1,2}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|P^{m}B_{m,i}-B_{m,i}\|_{1,2}\\
&=\sum_{m\in[p]}\|P^{m}B_{m}-B_{m}\|_{1,2}\end{split}

(91)

Here the first inequality is by Lemma
29
. The second and third inequalities are by conditioning on
\mathcal{E}
, since
P^{m}
is an orthogonal projection matrix of rank at most
O(k\log^{2}k)
. The fourth inequality is because, for any matrix
M
,
\|M\|_{F}\leq\|M\|_{1,2}
. The first equality is by the definition
B_{m,i}:=M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}
, and the second equality is by the definition
B_{m}:=[B_{m,1},\ldots,B_{m,n}]
(and the fact that the
\ell_{1,2}
norm decomposes across columns).

If we condition on
\mathcal{G}
, then since
12NormApprox
is an
O(1)
-approximation algorithm, there exists an absolute constant such that for all
m\in[m]
and for all rank-
k
projection matrices
Q^{m}
,

\|P^{m}B_{m}-B_{m}\|_{1,2}\leq C\|Q^{m}B_{m}-B_{m}\|_{1,2}

Thus, if
r
is the number of rows in
T_{m,i}
for
m\in[p]
and
i\in[n]
, then

\begin{split}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|P^{m}B_{m}-B_{m}\|_{1,2}&\lesssim\sum_{m\in[p]}\|Q^{m}B_{m}-B_{m}\|_{1,2}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|Q^{m}B_{m,i}-B_{m,i}\|_{1,2}\\
&=\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|Q^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}-M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}\|_{1,2}\\
&\lesssim\sqrt{r}\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|Q^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}-M_{m}(A^{i})S_{m,i}^{T}T_{m,i}^{T}\|_{F}\\
&\lesssim\sqrt{r}\sum_{i\in[n]}\sum_{m\in[p]}\|Q^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}\end{split}

(92)

Here, the first inequality is because for any matrix
M
with
r
columns,
\|M\|_{1,2}\leq\sqrt{r}\|M\|_{F}
. The second inequality is by our assumption that
\mathcal{E}
holds.

Thus, combining Equations
91
and
92
, there is an absolute constant
C
such that for all rank-
k
projection matrices
Q^{1},\ldots,Q^{p}
,

\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{F}\leq C\sqrt{r}\sum_{m\in[p]}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|Q^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}

Taking the infimum over all
Q^{1},\ldots,Q^{p}
on the right-hand side gives

\begin{split}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|(P^{1}\times\ldots\times P^{p}&\times I_{n}\times\ldots\times I_{n})\text{vec}(A^{i})-\text{vec}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&\leq C\sqrt{r}\min_{Q^{1},\ldots,Q^{p}}\sum_{m\in[p]}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|Q^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&=C\sqrt{r}\sum_{m\in[p]}\min_{Q^{m}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\|Q^{m}M_{m}(A^{i})-M_{m}(A^{i})\|_{F}\\
&\leq C\sqrt{r}\sum_{m\in[p]}\mathcal{OPT}\\
&\leq Cp\sqrt{r}\cdot\mathcal{OPT}\\
&\leq O(p\cdot(\sqrt{k}\log k+\sqrt{\log(np)}))\cdot\mathcal{OPT}\end{split}

(93)

where the second inequality is by Lemma
28
. Thus, Algorithm
10
obtains an
O(p\cdot(\sqrt{k}\log k+\sqrt{\log(np)}))
approximation factor, and obtains a bicriteria rank of
O(k\log^{2}k)
on each mode.

Let us now analyze the running time of Algorithm
10
. First, for a single
m\in[p]
, let us determine the running time needed to find
\widehat{U^{m}}
. For
i\in[n]
,
B_{m,i}
can be computed in
\text{nnz}(A^{i})+n\cdot O(pnk^{2}\log^{4}k)\cdot O(k\log^{2}k+\log(np))=\text{nnz}(A^{i})+n^{2}\log n\cdot\text{poly}(pk)
. Thus,
B_{m}
can be computed in
\text{nnz}(A)+n^{3}\log n\cdot\text{poly}(pk)
. Next, observe that
B_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\cdot O(k\log^{2}k+\log(np))}
. Thus, the running time needed for one single trial of
\textsc{12NormApprox}(B_{m},k)
is at most
n^{2}\log^{2}n\cdot\text{poly}(pk)
, and the time needed for
O(\log p)
trials is
n^{2}\log^{2}n\cdot\text{poly}(pk)
as well. Thus, the overall running time needed to find
\widehat{U^{m}}
is
\text{nnz}(A)+n^{3}\log n\cdot\text{poly}(pk)
, and the overall running time of Algorithm
10
is
O(p\cdot\text{nnz}(A))+n^{3}\log n\cdot\text{poly}(pk)
.
∎

Remark 11
.

The assumption that
p<q
seems to be required for our technique to work. In other words, the factor
U^{q}
must be an identity matrix.

Appendix G
Additional Related Work

In this section, we discuss more related works mentioned earlier and compare them to our work in more detail.

The work of
[
SGL+20
]
presents a family of algorithms that performs sketching based on Tucker-decompositions by applying dimensionality reduction to each factor matrix independently. They give a
2
-pass algorithm, proving a
2q
-approximation ratio with constant probability for rank-
k
approximation for large enough
k
.

[
RR20
]
on CP decomposition and Tensor Train decomposition using randomized projections. There are similar prior results, such as
[
MB18
]
, that compute a subspace embedding for tensors. However, in many cases, this is too computationally expensive. For example, in
[
RR20
]
, the cost of computing the embedding alone is
O(kNd\max(R,\tilde{R})^{3})
, where
\tilde{R}
is the actual best CP or TT rank of the tensor. The results are exponential in the number of modes.

[
MSK20
]
builds upon Higher-Order SVD to perform lower rank approximation under the Tucker decomposition. The approximation error achieved was characterized by the discarded singular values of the matricizations of the tensor along different modes, which gives an
O(\sqrt{q-1})
approximation.

[
Ose11
]
defined Tensor Train decomposition and analyzed the TT-SVD algorithm. It incrementally finds an orthonormal bases for each factor matrix in the Tensor Train by finding the space for the top
k
singular values and flattening along each mode.

[
HSW17
]
modifies the original TT-SVD algorithm for Tensor Train decomposition studied in
[
Ose11
]
by performing a Gaussian sketch at each step. This introduces a
\sqrt{q-1}
dependence in the error term, but is significantly more efficient.
[
HSW17
]
only characterizes their result with singular values of matricizations and numerical experiments, and does not show their algorithm gives a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation.

[
LYB19
]
proposes an algorithm that uses a similar structure with TT-SVD and unlike most works, does achieve a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-relative error Tensor Train decomposition. However, their algorithm is very computationally expensive, and does not have a good guarantee of the rank of the output. This is because for each flattening, the algorithm sets
\delta=\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d-1}}
, and finds a
\delta-
truncated SVD for that matricization. This requires searching for the target rank, which is computationally expensive.

[
Han12
]
proposes algorithms to convert a tensor with low Tensor Train rank to a tensor with low Tensor Ring rank, and vice versa. This work also proposes an algorithm to convert a PEPS tensor network to a Tensor Train network. One key difference between our work and
[
Han12
]
is that we give a technique to convert an arbitrary tensor network (which can be structured according to any graph) to a tree of low rank. Our algorithm is different from that of
[
Han12
]
— while they contract various edges of the network and stop when the network has become a tree, we contract the original graph
\mathcal{G}
until it is a single vertex, but additionally form two new vertices each time we contract an edge. Finally, the algorithm of
[
Han12
]
can increase the rank by a factor as large as
n^{O(q)}
compared to our result.

Other traditional sketching and regression techniques, including leverage score sampling, have also been used to achieve better results in tensor approximation.
[
FGF21
]
applies ridge leverage score sampling regression to the Tucker decomposition problem. This technique is based on the earlier work on Kronecker product regression
[
DJS+19
]
. However,
[
FGF21
]
takes all the components of the Tucker decomposition as input and only solves for the core tensor.

[
MB21
]
uses leverage score sampling to obtain an approximation in the Tensor Ring format (a decomposition similar to Tensor Train, except that the first and the last mode are also contracted).

In the concurrent and independent work
[
MS22
]
,
the goal is to preserve the
\ell_{2}
norm of a vector which has tensor network structure.
[
MS22
]
gives embeddings which are tensor networks where each core has i.i.d. Gaussian entries. This is in the same spirit as our embeddings. For Tensor Train decomposition, the embeddings we use can be written as Tensor Trains where each core is formed from a Countsketch matrix, and for tree networks, our embeddings are tree networks (with the same structure as the desired tree network) whose cores are formed from Countsketch matrices. Our analysis is similar, as in
[
MS22
]
, the embeddings are also decomposed into a sequence of sketching matrices, which are Kronecker products of a Gaussian matrix and an identity matrix. Our embeddings themselves are different however, as their embedding consists of a Kronecker product of Gaussian matrices (in order to reduce the dimension of the uncontracted edges of the input tensor) followed by a binary tree-structured network of Gaussian tensors. The structure of the binary tree is determined by a given contraction order for the input tensor. On the other hand, our embedding for tree networks is a tree network but is not a binary tree in general.
[
MS22
]
also gives embeddings for general tensor networks which are not trees, while we do not consider this. We note that
[
MS22
]
has the goal of obtaining a tensor network embedding which satisfies the conclusion of the Johnson Lindenstrauss lemma, and does not consider low rank approximation of tensors.
